blue-card answer. – i am always in favour of taking steps forward when it comes to green policies, because i am among those in this house who believe that the future lies on the green path, which is beneficial for our industries and economies, as well as for jobs. however, we are not speaking about the commission’s working programme at the moment; we are reviewing the italian presidency. i think more could have been done, but actually much was also achieved if we take the circumstances into account: a new parliament, a new commission and also the devastating effects of the financial crisis that we are in. but that does not mean that we have an excuse not to do the right things on the green path.
mr president, commission president juncker, prime minister renzi, you are all here! i would like to take this opportunity to thank the italian presidency for the very good work it has done in each area. after the events of the last few days in france, it seems almost foolish to discuss small matters such as trademark rules, but perhaps that is the strength of our union - that we can deal with big important issues and some minor ones at the same time. as a proponent of the reform of european trade marks, i have had the privilege of working with very skilled diplomats and experts. i am convinced that, with the great progress we have made, we will soon have a solution to this problem. the europe of the people was strengthened by the grief expressed by three and a half million citizens over the weekend. they expect us to defend openness now the open europe where fundamental rights are a cornerstone. that is why it is so important that we now establish and promote this in everything we do.
madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? ladies and gentlemen, the compromise is regrettable, but it is the only compromise possible, and i hope that it will be adopted. i have never been able, and none of us could ever be able, to defend the way in which gmo technology is being used today. it is not good for man or for the environment. it is only useful for the profits of the very large companies. never forget, though, that technology in itself is never good or bad. it depends on who's doing what. i want us in europe, above all, to take back control of this type of plant breeding from the united states.
madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? the amendment to the law we are voting on today allows member states to largely decide for themselves whether gmos are to be grown on their territory. i think it is a well-considered compromise and i shall vote for it. it respects our different attitudes and views on gmos. it allows the member states to take more decisions themselves. i think it's good in a matter like this that splits. there are different views, and it is important to respect the will of the people. for my part, i think we should listen very carefully to the science and to the evaluations that, for example, efsa is making. we also have to take the precautionary principle into account. it is important not to rush into difficult issues, or allow things that could have negative effects on human health and the environment. the agreement also provides for measures to deal with the cultivation of gmos, so-called buffer zones between member states. i think that's very important.
in writing. i voted against the proposal for several reasons. although the current fisheries agreements between the eu and non-eu countries are much better than in the past, especially for fish stocks, there are several problems. fisheries policy must have a functioning social dimension. instead, the eu continues to exploit the waters of developing countries. the alternative should be to help these countries build their own sustainable fishing industry. in addition, in this case são tomé and príncipe will receive extremely low compensation for fishing and the chances that this money will contribute to the local fisheries industry are low. the price will be eur 35 per tonne in year 1, rising to eur 70 per tonne in year 4 (compared to the eu-seychelles (mayotte) agreement where the price is eur 110 per tonne in year 1 and eur 6 125 per tonne in year 6). at the same time, there is a lack of reliable catch data for the area and the possibilities to monitor capacity are low. the prevalence of corruption in the region also leads to development funds being 'lost along the way'. finally, there is also shark hunting in the area. all in all, this led me to vote against the proposal.
in writing. i voted against the motion. this agreement is different in that it involves the eu giving fishing rights to another country. but that's problematic. local fishermen from mayotte, for example, have not been included in the negotiations, and even the committee's rapporteur is critical of this and of the fact that only 24 nautical miles are reserved for local fishing. mayotte should also be given the opportunity to develop its own sustainable fishing industry. for example, there is currently no infrastructure to deal with large-scale fisheries, which means that the local community is unlikely to benefit from this agreement. although france is called upon to use the european maritime and fisheries fund to create an appropriate administrative framework, monitoring activities, physical infrastructure and sufficient capacity to meet cfp requirements, none of these conditions is currently fulfilled. i am saying that this must be put in place before a fisheries agreement is concluded. the seychelles are also known for letting other countries fish under their flag. so i voted no.
madam president, i wonder why we have the european food security authority at all if we ignore the last twenty-five years of research, with millions of euros invested in assessing gmos. so far, no evidence shows risks for food security, animal welfare or health. i understand that many people are concerned, but policy in this house should be based on science, not on emotions. the debate has instead been carried by green ideology; it has created the fears that gmo is something artificial. by letting member states so easily opt out, we are fragmenting the single market. how does this go along with a common agricultural and food security policy? the answer has not been given. we need free choice, but for consumers. that is why it should be labelled if it is gmo or not. we need it for producers, so that all producers – and not only big companies – can produce gmos, rather than having member states opting out. science, research, development, technology, innovation – that is the way ahead in europe.
in writing. i voted against the report this with the intention of voting in favour of the original proposal by the committee on the environment. the starting point for this report was good, but in the very hurried tripartite talks many of the most important parts were removed, which has created a text which is on a weak legal basis and does not give member states an absolute possibility to decide for themselves whether to abandon gmo crops. the polluter pays principle is not included in the text and the change of the legal basis of the legislation from environment to internal market and the transfer of the precautionary principle in the text to a reason risk leakage of the precautionary principle in favour of competition issues and market access of gmo companies and further undermining the environmental perspective. although the proposal contains some improvements compared with the previous one, i have therefore voted against it.
legislation on gm crops must be based on a scientific assessment of risks and benefits. this has been a swedish position ever since we joined the eu 20 years ago, regardless of political colour. it is regrettable that the new swedish government has departed from the principle of scientificity when it chose to abstain from voting despite the green light given by efsa. the swedish government thus chose to base its policy on popular opinion rather than science. the fact that the government chose not to enshrine the principle decision in the eu council makes matters worse. the proposal for a national ban on gmos is another step away from scientificity which we cannot support. it risks fragmenting the internal market and thus also threatens the free movement which is the cornerstone of european cooperation. european legislation must be based on scientific and objective criteria.
in writing. — this report was misleadingly labelled as giving member states more powers. in actual fact, whilst amendment 20 did give powers back, this was cancelled out by the other 50 amendments which maintained and re-asserted eu control and authority over the whole procedure. the eu did not allow us to vote on individual amendments, which we regard as rather unfair. therefore, the sweden democrats could not support this block vote.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the resolution on libya which we adopted on 16 september describes the situation in the country in terms of a power vacuum, violence and political instability. today, those words are inadequate. today there is reason to speak of anarchy and civil war. meanwhile, another human catastrophe is unfolding. in 2014, the italian navy rescued around 100,000 refugees in the mediterranean, mainly from libyan ports. but thousands of the fugitives could not be saved, their fragile vessels never reaching the shores of europe. now, as we discuss libya, there are a couple of painful facts that deserve mention. there are no legal ways into europe today. the vulnerability of boat migrants is perhaps the most obvious and tragic consequence of this. the italian rescue operation mare nostrum had more than twice the resources of its successor, triton. the member states' lack of willingness to help is discouraging. it is our duty to speak out about this, about the lack of fair distribution of responsibility between the eu member states. only together, through common solidarity, can we solve this catastrophic refugee situation. for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. i think it is important that we have this debate tonight because we are continuously receiving, week after week, month after month, information about boats sinking in the mediterranean and people drowning. this is the message that haunts us. we politicians must now admit defeat, because people continue to die in the mediterranean and the mediterranean has become a sea of death, a gigantic cemetery without borders. human traffickers continue to find new ways to make even more money off desperate people. to find a solution, we must try to get to the bottom of the problem. it's about people fleeing war and persecution and finding themselves in desperate situations. there are no legal ways to get to europe. the only alternative is ruthless human traffickers who make a fortune off the misery of others. the smugglers have a monopoly on the market and will continue to exploit people's desperation to make money as long as we do not take our responsibility towards humanity. we cannot continue to refer to third countries. we have to be part of it, too. our mission must be to take away the smugglers' business idea. only then will we put an end to these voyages across the sea. it is by creating legal, safe routes to europe, increased resettlement, humanitarian visas issued at embassies and consulates, in refugee zones and refugee camps. because getting here safely is not the same as opening the doors to a mass influx of migrants, as some critics seem to believe. those who come here are subject to the same asylum process as everyone else, and those who have reasons are allowed to stay and those who don't are not allowed to stay. it is never up to anyone else to solve europe's difficult problems. we have to take responsibility and do it now because it's on fire.

madam president, our answer to the mounting and multiple challenges in the mediterranean is not to close the borders but to open safe and legal ways for refugees while combating criminality and abuse. citizens have legitimate concerns, too many are coming too fast, but populism is never the answer. our target should be the smugglers and not the refugees. our response to the ruthless criminals who abandon children, women and men in ghost boats should be clear: increase security cooperation, increase cooperation with third countries like turkey, strengthen frontex, triton and the eurosur coordination system with more resources. all member states should assist the mediterranean states facing this emergency, and i commend italy for the ‘mare nostrum’ policy. but solidarity should also be with the countries that welcome most of the asylum seekers, like sweden. more member states should take more responsibility for more refugees, including with resettlement. the new common rules in the asylum package should be implemented by all. the time has come to change the way we look at refugees. yes, in the short term they are a cost; but in the long term, let us be honest, we need them. we do not say ‘go home’; we say ‘go to work’. integration has to go hand in hand with asylum, and i am proud that europe is a humanitarian superpower. we should continue to protect people fleeing from war and terror.
madam president, i would like to thank you. when we talk about international organized crime, we make a clear distinction between human smuggling, which is the subject of this discussion, and human trafficking and trafficking in human beings. the most important difference is in how we view the victims. we have made progress in combating trafficking in human beings and protecting those who fall victim to such trafficking. there is a european convention and an eu directive that sets a common standard. however, it does not seem to be in the interest of the european union to discuss how to protect victims of human trafficking. there is a huge legal and political vacuum here. the eu contributes to the exploitation of people in the mediterranean by a restrictive migration policy and by focusing on policing borders rather than on saving lives and providing protection. as a consequence of the eu's border policy, people are forced to turn to smugglers and are seen not as exploited but as illegals or even criminals themselves. this has to stop. we have to see them as victims. between 2007 and 2013, the eu spent €2 billion on strengthening the surveillance of the eu's external borders, and only €700 million on improving the situation of refugees and asylum seekers. we should redistribute resources to improve the situation of refugees and create safe routes to the eu for all. it's the only way to stop the market for human trafficking.

for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. a fundamental pillar of our european rule of law is the right to have a confidential and free conversation with one's legal representative, one's lawyer. article 4 of the 2013 directive on the right to a lawyer, as well as the charter of fundamental human rights, also states this very clearly. if our intelligence services then collect information between lawyers and their clients, this fundamental principle is put at risk and, in the long run, the rule of law in the eu is undermined in a totally unacceptable way. it now appears that the dutch law firm prakken d'oliveira has been under surveillance for a number of years by the dutch security services. there are many other similar examples in the member states. i would now like to know whether the commission and the council consider that this kind of espionage against law firms is compatible with european law, the treaties, the charter of rights and all the rights which they confer on us as citizens. because if that were the case, if you really think that this falls within the framework of national security, then i would really like to know how you define the concept of 'national security' and if you think that this should automatically lead to european citizens being deprived of their rights. now i would like to ask the following questions, first of all: what measures do the commission and the council intend to take to ensure that confidential contacts between lawyers and their clients are respected? secondly: will you take any concrete action against the netherlands? thirdly: when will you return to parliament with an acceptable definition of the concept of 'national security'?

what lessons have been learned from the recent pilot project? my second question: how does the commission intend to ensure compliance with the principles set out in the 1992 council recommendation – i.e. no substitution of wages, no discrimination and no conflict with participation in decision-making? my third question: how does the commission intend to promote best practice for employee ownership schemes? fourth question: how does the commission plan to ensure employee participation and the inclusion of workers’ representatives in the planning and implementation of such schemes? fifth question: does the commission intend to develop models of strategic ownership as regards employees’ shares in eso schemes so as to serve the long-term interests of companies and their employees? sixth and last question: what measures should be taken to protect employees’ savings invested in a company, in the event of a crisis or of the company becoming insolvent, so as to avoid the double risk of employees losing their job, income and savings at the same time? a comprehensive involvement of employees and their representatives requires proper information concerning the underlying risks of joining a participation scheme and also raises questions related to taxation and labour law. how can the commission ensure that workers are not being used, that all employees obtain equal access, and that they never have to face the risk of financial ruin should the company concerned run into insolvency?

in writing. — i voted in favour of the european parliament resolution on the us senate report on the use of torture by the cia. i believe that we have to take a strong stand and condemn without hesitation the use of torture. it is unfortunate, however, that in the resolution the tone and language are at times anti-american. i absolutely do not share this approach. the us is our partner and ally and i welcome that the obama administration has clearly condemned and stopped those practices. i also did not support the request for a new committee of inquiry on the alleged transportation and illegal detention of prisoners in europe by the cia, as there are already ongoing investigations in various member states.
in writing. today we voted against the joint resolution on counter-terrorism put forward by the four major political groups in this parliament. the green group has tabled its own resolution on counter-terrorism measures, which we voted for. while we are in favour of coordinated action against terrorism, we consider the joint resolution to be ineffective. the mass surveillance proposed has few known positive results. the proposals ignore more long-term preventive measures and neglect fundamental rights. to be effective, a counter-terrorism strategy should focus on targeted surveillance of suspects, ensuring that authorities have the necessary means and resources and targeting specific risks and concrete suspicions. better information exchange between these authorities is also needed. radicalisation in schools and prisons must be tackled. the joint resolution is therefore merely symbolic, giving the impression that we are giving concrete answers to the legitimate demands of the citizens to feel safe and secure.
in writing. i voted against the resolution. the overall reason for this is the resolution's clear focus on repressive and police measures. i want us to evaluate what instruments are available in the eu today before new laws and measures are proposed. i do not want the paris attacks to become the european equivalent of 9/11. the united states' 15-year war on terror has been proven a total failure. the resolution calls for a number of measures which are being taken at the expense of the integrity and democratic freedoms of the eu and which, above all, do not represent any real solutions. these include the eu-pnr. the resolution lacks a deeper understanding of how to counter radicalisation in our societies and fails to take into account at all how segregation, exclusion and increasing divisions, and islamophobia are creating fertile ground. the resolution also lacks criticism of the failure of the eu and its member states' foreign policies.

mr president, i would like to thank you. people care about what they eat. that is why we have been pushing for clear information, understandable information and origin labelling of all fresh meat. it's now. however, the commission clearly shows that further labelling of ingredients is not feasible. it would increase prices by 9 percent and it would increase the administrative burden on businesses by 30 percent, and consumers are not prepared to pay for it. it is also misleading to claim that it would counteract cheating. mislabelling should be considered a crime and we need deterrent sanctions, we need stronger controls and we need to enforce the rules that exist. more rules are not the answer. my proposal is to improve the voluntary labelling system in place to increase consumer choice and the ability of businesses to profile themselves. that is why i voted against. i think there will be innovation and technology that will increase transparency and traceability, like the epr code. it is high time to bring out the real consumer voices. additional labelling does not protect consumers. i'm gonna keep saying no more food fraud.
today the european parliament voted on the origin marking of meat in processed foods. in the past, the european parliament has supported rules which meant that clear and comprehensible information and mandatory origin labelling should cover all fresh meat. these are the rules, and i stand by them. the commission has been clear that additional labelling of ingredients in processed foods is not feasible. it would raise food prices by up to 9% and the administrative burden on businesses by 30%. this would be a blow to many consumers and it is misleading to claim that it prevents fraud. mislabelling should be considered a criminal offence, to be met with deterrent sanctions, stronger controls and implementation of existing rules. i voted for the epp amendment on a voluntary labelling system, to increase consumer choice and the ability of companies to profile themselves.
in writing. we humans have a genetic and cultural heritage that makes us want to know what we are eating, so it is only natural that we become anxious about not knowing what is in the food packaging. the consumer's free, informed choice is important to us. we have therefore supported the resolution calling on the european commission to table a legislative proposal for mandatory labelling of meat in processed foods - something that 90% of european consumers say they want - especially as the resolution so clearly emphasises the need for expanded and strengthened checks to ensure that consumer information is actually accurate. we also believe, as is also reflected in the text of the resolution, that any legislative proposal must of course be practicable. a law that is not followed or that does not work could just as easily have been written in water.

we need also to join forces on combating the trafficking of people, and undertake increased cooperation with countries like turkey. some people here talk about fortress europe. again, it is irresponsible. we are talking about combating criminals who are smuggling women and children fleeing for their lives. the key word here is more responsibility amongst all member states, including responding to the desperate call of unhcr for a resettlement programme. i conclude by saying that we have the tools. we should be able to live up to being a humanitarian superpower if all the member states take responsibility as they should.

the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the i would like to ask mrs andersson a question. it's a massive critique of what's called "megatrucks". let me say it again. i'm from sweden. i've been working in a truck shed for 30 years as a truck driver. it is not the big lorries that we are concerned about in terms of road fatalities - it is your small cars from europe, as i like to call them. your 40-tonne, two-axle european tractors that don't really work on our road network. as for road damage, i'd like to say: do the math and see what it's all about. a 60-tonne car with 26 tires and seven axles has a ground pressure of 2,300 kilograms. a 40-ton car has 12 tires, five axles and a ground pressure of 3,300 kilograms, that is, one ton more in ground pressure per tire. which car do you think is doing the most damage to the infrastructure?
in writing. we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we regard as an expression of protectionism. we have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the exclusion that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in the light of the recent analysis by the swedish national audit office, which considers that the fund does indeed have a negative effect on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its eligibility and should not be used any more.

in writing. ‒ i firmly support the istanbul convention regarding the elimination of female genital mutilation (fgm), as well as the resolutionʼs call for member states to prevent domestic and sexual violence against women and girls. women must have control over their sexual and reproductive health; this includes the access to contraception and abortion, but this control must also apply to men. i acknowledge the importance of active prevention, education and information policies aimed at, amongst others, teenagers, but this must apply equally to young girls and young men. however, i do not believe that parliament should impose gender equality by using gender quotas or by promoting through campaigns that girls enter traditionally male careers. i want to underline that it is important for me that each family decides on its own work-life balance, family structure and parental leave sharing. forcing children and young people to follow educational studies based on gender or making them fight so called ʽgender stereotypesʼ, as mentioned in the resolution is, from my point of view, a violation of each individualʼs right to choose his/her personal future.
in writing. – i firmly support the istanbul convention regarding the elimination of female genital mutilation (fgm), as well as the resolutionʼs call for the member states to prevent domestic and sexual violence against women and girls. women must have control over their sexual and reproductive health; this includes access to contraception and abortion, but this control must also apply to men. i do acknowledge that active prevention, education and information policies aimed at, amongst others, teenagers are of great importance, but they must target equally young girls and young men. however, i do not believe that the ep should impose gender equality by using gender quotas or promoting through campaigns girls entering what are traditionally male careers. i want to underline that it is important for me that each family decides about its own work-life balance, family structure and parental leave sharing. forcing children and young people to follow educational studies based on gender or making them fight so-called ʽgender stereotypesʼ, as mentioned in the resolution is, from my point of view, a violation of each individualʼs right to choose his/her personal future.

in writing. - i voted against the guidelines. the report states that the eu budget is a tool for internal solidarity by supporting economic, social and territorial cohesion, contributing to the fight against poverty, promoting social inclusion and helping to minimise development disparities between member states. for millions of people, particularly in southern europe, who are unemployed and forced to live in poverty and misery because of the eu's austerity policies, the report's allegations are a mockery. these guidelines initiate the process for the 2016 budget. my basic criticism is that the eu budget is too large and too comprehensive. there should be major cuts. i also think it is wrong in principle for the european parliament to have power over the budget. the member states that pay the eu budget should have full say on the union's expenditure. other reasons why i am opposed to the eu budget are the costly and environmentally damaging agricultural policy, the unmanageable regional and structural funds, where there is widespread fraud, and the frontex efforts, which mean continuing to build fortress europe. these are some of the reasons why i am voting against the guidelines for the 2016 budget.
‒ we cannot support the european parliament's demand for a larger eu budget. instead of prioritising spending, the european parliament is demanding more budgetary space to meet political declarations and unpaid bills. nor can we support the own resources reform demanded by the european parliament, which in practice means giving the eu taxing rights. a larger eu budget and taxation rights are not the solution to europe's problems. structural reforms, deregulation and reduction of bureaucracy are what are strengthening the eu's competitiveness and creating the conditions for growth and jobs. we also do not support the exclusion of certain payments from the ceiling for payments in the eu's multiannual budget. dilution of budgetary requirements is unlikely to create the conditions for growth. instead, the eu must prioritise spending and focus on results and efficiency. we therefore vote against the european parliament's report on the establishment of the general guidelines for the establishment of the 2016 budget.

for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. i regret, as does my colleague claude moraes, that this debate is taking place when the chamber is empty, when the journalists have gone home to sleep or eat, listen to music or read a book. i regret that this important statement by the commission, perhaps the most important statement the commission has made in a very long time, is not receiving the attention it deserves. the recent anti-semitic attacks in paris and copenhagen bear witness and speak their own clear language. in my own country, sweden, where we have a fairly good reputation internationally for the protection of minorities, jews do not feel safe today. the media and opinion leaders are helping to reinforce the anti-jewish climate, and many are not taken seriously when they express concern. a swedish journalist asked a policeman in malmö some time ago what the police were doing to combat attacks on jews, such as the rabbi in malmö, who has been repeatedly beaten and subjected to physical violence. the police then responded that the rabbi is so self-sacrificing, referring to the rabbi's wearing kippah and star of david and jewish symbols. it has gone very, very far in europe when the very police force that is supposed to defend the right of every person to be who they are, sees it as a problem that someone is jewish. many jews in sweden and other european countries are afraid today. they want to leave europe. it is no longer the europe we live in. they have been met with misunderstanding and accusations. we have all failed to provide security and to protect religious freedom, and we must admit that today. it's happening right in front of our eyes. we must stop turning a blind eye to the reality facing our religious minorities today.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank mr cadec, who has tabled this resolution, who has taken the initiative. the sea bass, one of our most magnificent predatory fish, is severely depleted. today, the fishing mortality rate for sea bass is four times higher than the level that could ensure maximum sustainable yield, msy. we are talking about a fish that grows very slowly and does not reach sexual maturity until it is four to seven years old. the international council for the exploration of the sea (ices) warns that a management plan should be put in place as soon as possible and that the fishery should be reduced by 80 percent. today, the catch of sea bass is not regulated at all in the eu. there are no limits on maximum allowable catches, nor are there any quotas. the only restriction in principle in commercial fishing is a minimum of 36 cm. but commercial fishing is not the only activity. it is estimated that 25 percent of the total catch is made by recreational fishermen. the council has not been able to agree on measures. instead, the commission has been given the opportunity to apply the emergency brake and to suspend part of the trawl fishery until the end of april. it's time to take responsibility for the situation before it's too late. the commission should prepare a proposal for a management plan without delay. that management plan should take reasonable account of all catches of hake, both commercial and recreational. the fair management of a limited common resource must reasonably include all those who use it. then we'll have a government for the good of all.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is a very important resolution that we are facing, especially at this time of great turmoil around the world. i would like to go back to 1948, when the universal declaration of human rights was born as a lesson of world war and the holocaust. since then, a string of conventions has been added to enable individuals to live freely and equally in mutual respect for one another. the rights are universal and interdependent and should not be based on arbitrariness. the final declaration of the declaration forbids us as individuals, as groups and as states to actively oppose its contents. today, despite the hopes of previous generations that their mistakes will not be repeated, we actively oppose human rights both within and outside the member states every day. rights alone are no guarantee. we humans are the ones who can guarantee it. it is therefore up to us to uphold human rights. we must take action against the increasing discriminatory laws and violations against people on the grounds of sexual orientation or gender identity in europe and worldwide. human rights for women as well as for lgbtq people, refugees, migrants and children are all equally fundamental to peace and democracy. male violence against women is one of the world's greatest security problems. women's rights to education and health, and sexual and reproductive rights, are fundamental human rights. the european parliament must actively work towards the implementation of the beijing platform, adopted 20 years ago. this resolution reaffirms our determination to achieve human rights in all areas. we have made a commitment. we must stand up for what we have committed ourselves to. it's our duty.

this is marita ulvskog, the interviewer. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. one of my strongest memories of the eu election campaign last year is a phone call from a priest in sweden. she expressed her despair over the social misery that prevailed in the large parking lot outside her town. there are lines of trucks lined up, waiting for new runs. meanwhile, the drivers live in their cars. they have difficulty with food and hygiene, light fires in parking lots or warm themselves in small liquor stores in the swedish winter cold. they have no salary, no collective agreement just their waiting. this is unworthy. the agricultural industry has a major problem with unfair competition. from countries with lower wages and worse conditions, permanent domestic traffic is carried to countries with higher wages and better conditions. people are forced to work under unacceptable conditions, while serious transport companies are being shut down. most member states have collectively negotiated or statutory minimum wage scales, including for the transport sector. some member states also apply them to, for example, foreign hauliers, in terms of minimum wages and administrative requirements, such as prior authorisation, when they carry out road haulage abroad, in transit operations such as international transport or cabotage. i therefore ask the commission, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, the following questions: first, what provisions of eu and national law does the commission consider apply to truck drivers and other workers providing services while travelling through or in another member state? what is the situation regarding the terms and conditions of employment and the applicable law in the territory of the transit or host member state? to what extent may domestic rules apply to foreign transport operations? question two: what is the commission's view of such transport activities in terms of the terms and conditions of employment and the law applicable to the employment relationship at the time of the provision of the service, including the posting of workers directive and the rome i regulation? question three: what measures does the commission intend to take to protect the social rights of workers in the road transport sector? the eu's objective of achieving a deregulated internal market will never be achieved unless the commission also has the courage to recognise the problems, the very serious problems, that eu legislation has created. as the commission prepares a major review of europe's transport rules, the problems can no longer be ignored. instead of further liberalisation, measures against social dumping and unfair competition must therefore be put in place. it will be crucial to the content of the eu project, both in terms of the regulatory framework and the democratic content.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have something that none of you, my colleagues, have here, namely 30 years of experience behind the wheel of a lorry. i know exactly what the problems are, i know why they have arisen, and i see the situation getting worse. in sweden, the market is flooded with cars driving for reimbursements that are well below the limit of what a swedish company can do. the cars are manned by drivers who may be paid as little as 500 euros a month. that, ladies and gentlemen, is what you have in two days' treatment! you even have more in your allowance than they have in a month. what is happening is scandalous and it is all happening under the protection of the eu's cabotage regulation, which is full of loopholes. what's happening on our roads is simply not decent. is minimum wage a way forward? i don't know, but swedish companies go bankrupt on a regular basis. drivers are fired, taxpayers' money is lost. fair competition is not feared, but that situation does not exist now. then you hear the defense that the drivers of the foreign cars have a salary that is completely unfair compared to what they earn in their home country. what good is that? they are not in their home country. they have a very different cost of living. the question now is what the commission intends to do to put this problem in order. some call it protectionism and nationalism when you try to protect your country's companies and employees. i call it morality and decency to demand fair conditions on our roads and in our workplaces. the social dumping must be stopped. what does the commission propose to achieve this? there must be some kind of goddamn order in the transport sector!
president-in-office of the council.– madam president, it is a privilege to address this session today, and today we are discussing an issue that goes to the heart of european values, of who we are and what we stand for. so, as we prepare to celebrate international roma day on 8 april, it is opportune to reflect on the situation of europe’s largest ethnic minority, on their circumstances, and on progress made and the challenges ahead. not only is it opportune, but it is also necessary because europe’s success in improving the situation of roma communities is a measure of our ability to promote integration and tackle social exclusion everywhere. that is why four years ago the council established the eu framework for national roma integration strategies. this framework invited member states to come forward with strategies to promote roma inclusion in four key areas – education, employment, healthcare and housing. and just over a year ago, on 9 december 2013, the council added fresh impetus to this drive by adopting a recommendation on effective roma integration measures. there has been progress. member states have stepped up their efforts and are taking action. the european parliament has been very instrumental, by keeping the topic on the agenda, by assisting with ideas and discussion, as well as by reminding the council and its member states of the need to further progress on the issue. but this is a long road and major challenges remain. it is regrettable that in 2015 many roma still live in conditions that are simply unacceptable. they still face deep poverty and social marginalisation. many lack basic access to housing and healthcare. the unemployment rate is above the eu average, and roma children are less likely to finish school. according to the eu fundamental rights agency, as many as 54% of roma still experience discrimination when looking for employment. too often, roma are the victims of prejudice, intolerance and social exclusion. joint eu and national level action is helping, but genuine progress, the sort that will help not just future generations of roma but also this generation, requires something much more fundamental than what is in place today: a change in public attitudes towards roma communities. that is far easier said than done. there are three concrete steps that we can take to improve public awareness and understanding, all of which are included in the council’s recommendation of december 2013. first, tackling prejudice and discrimination head on. this is a no-brainer and true for every group facing intolerance. we must take effective measures to combat anti-gypsyism, negative rhetoric and hate speech. we must fight against racist and stigmatising language. second, raising public awareness about roma people. as we all know, intolerance is a common product of misunderstanding. so improving understanding of roma communities, not just of the problems they face, but also of their history and rich culture, has to be part of the solution. roma culture is an inherent part of the cultural diversity of the european union. how aware, for example, are ordinary europeans of the roma genocide during the second world war? fostering public awareness of roma history and culture, especially the traumatic and tragic events of the second world war, is one of the most powerful ways in which we can support social cohesion and fight exclusion. this learning must start in our schools and universities and be nurtured across our societies. that also includes the media, which has a vital role to play. third, and perhaps most urgently, we need to empower the roma community, to give them a real platform to speak out for themselves, and to actively engage in public life as full eu citizens. these steps are key strands of the council’s recommendation of december 2013. i note with great pleasure that they are also important issues for parliament. being a politician myself, i am convinced that parliamentarians play a significant role in shaping society’s attitude. the european parliament plays a particularly important role in promoting tolerance. with international roma day almost upon us, we must celebrate the contribution made by roma to europe’s cultural and historical tapestry, and we must redouble our commitment to tackling the social and economic challenges they face. we all need to work hand-in-hand towards full eradication of discrimination, towards raising public awareness and empowering roma communities. only then can we really make progress on this pressing agenda. i will conclude by paraphrasing a famous advertisement. let us strive towards the union, which helps people help themselves!
member of the commission.– madam president, honourable members of parliament, thank you for giving me a chance to speak to you today on a subject which is often overlooked. on 2 august 1944 almost 3 000 elderly people, women and children – the remaining sinti and roma of the so-called zigeunerlager in the nazi concentration camp of auschwitz-birkenau – were murdered in the gas chambers. the atrocities against roma and sinti during the second world war must not be forgotten. this debate is a very small acknowledgement of a terrible debt. a moral debt to the roma and sinti people, wiped off the face of the earth during the second world war, and wiped from our memories after the second world war. commemorating the roma and sinti victims is our duty. and we have to remain vigilant. extremists are becoming more visible and their audience bigger. hate speech is getting louder, hate crime more frequent. europe’s old demons are attempting a comeback. we must confront them. fifteen years ago we put in place anti-discrimination directives, yet roma are still victims of discrimination. we have legislation in place combating racism and xenophobia, yet roma are still victims of racism and xenophobia. i agree that anti-gypsyism, as a specific form of racism, must be of serious concern to all of us. a lot has been done at national and european level over the past few years to foster the integration of roma. in particular, the adoption of the eu framework for national roma integration strategies in 2011 initiated a change in roma integration policies in the member states. but although we have put in place comprehensive integration strategies and made funding available, and despite our efforts to promote diversity and to highlight the benefits of roma integration, roma are still victims of economic and social exclusion and we still see very few real improvements on the ground. we need consistency and coherence in our action. the commission monitors member states’ compliance with the racial equality directive, which prohibits discrimination on grounds of racial or ethnic origin in a number of specific fields, including education. the commission is ready to take action whenever member states are found to be in breach of their duties under the directive. deeply rooted stereotypes and prejudices link roma to criminality, anti-social behaviour or benefit dependency. but how can we dismantle such stereotypes and prejudices? first, by applying the principle of equality before the law. roma are eu citizens, and as such they should enjoy the same rights and have the same duties as the rest of us. second, by ensuring equal opportunities. this is the fundamental objective behind our action – to build societies which offer equal opportunities to all, including to roma. the 2013 council recommendation on roma integration requests that member states implement measures to combat discrimination and anti-gypsyism. to be effective, fighting discrimination should be mainstreamed into all policies, including on education, employment and access to services. it starts with basic education. roma children must not be segregated. teaching roma children from socially excluded communities to read, count and write is necessary, but it is not sufficient to create that special feeling of belonging to a society. i expect the member states to raise awareness about the benefits of roma integration, both in roma communities and among the general public. they could address the inclusion problems that roma face in public curricula and teaching materials. we will carefully monitor these aspects in the member states’ reports about their implementation measures at the end of this year. there are policies and legal and financial tools in place, both at national and eu level. we need to work on their combined enforcement in order to achieve sustainable results. we need to join forces and assume our roles and responsibilities in this process. we need to ensure that the solutions are discussed and implemented at local level, since this is where the exclusion takes place. this is, at least, what i take as the main message from last week’s european platform for roma inclusion meeting. another outcome is the importance of building trust among stakeholders in roma integration at national level. therefore, national roma platforms should be set up, and we will work on this together with the national roma contact points. europe’s tragic past serves as a reminder of our duties and responsibilities towards the future. i believe our debate today will raise greater awareness and send a strong signal against anti-gypsyism and all forms of racism.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. madam president and commissioner jourová! the situation of roma in the eu member states is, as it is today, under criticism. every day we violate fundamental human rights and the fundamental values of the eu. tens of millions of people live in great exclusion every day, in all areas. discrimination, hate crime and poverty are effects of anti-gypsyism. this is based solely on roma ethnicity. anti-gypsyism, anti-gypsyism, which is directed against roma has been doing so since the early 15th century. it is time to take a strong political stand in this very urgent situation. we should be ashamed. it is not okay that people today in a modern society, because of ethnicity, still cannot have a vision of their future, but only have a daily survival strategy. as a first step in the fight against anti-gypsyism, we in this elected house are voting unanimously for a resolution on the recognition of 2 august as the day of remembrance of the genocide of the roma. this day is already being observed by the roma community. zigeunernacht between 2 and 3 august, auschwitz/birkenau was emptied of any remaining roma. at 9:00 a.m. on august 2, the list looked like this: 2,987 persons. at 9:00 on august 3rd, it was 0. witnesses say the smell and stench from the crematorium never wanted to end. during the nuremberg trials, no mention was made of roma. no roman could testify. this year marks the 70th anniversary of the liberation of the auschwitz/birkenau concentration camps. i think it's high time we gave the romans a little recognition for august 2. it is of great symbolic value for the roma of europe, but also for democracy itself. it is about recognising history and aiming for a future built on the eu's fundamental values. i would point out that this resolution has no political colour. it is the duty of all of us who stand for democratic values to vote in favour of this resolution in april.
for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. the last step in genocide is considered to be denial. it is therefore important that we now, on the eve of international roma day, speak out clearly about what has been too much minimized for too long, namely the genocide of roma during the second world war. it was the same time, the same war, the same administration, the same way into the gas chambers - whether you were roma, jewish, gay, disabled, etc. the holocaust is about all the victims of the nazi death camps. as a people without a state, the roma are found in all our countries. they are citizens like us and part of our societies, but they were never, and are not, allowed to speak for themselves. the most basic rights of roma are still being violated in many european countries. they are discriminated against and persecuted. the majority society speaks about roma and for roma. we think we know what's best for them, and we dictate their terms. we continue to divide people into categories based on ethnicity, sexual orientation, etc. that we, in 2015, 70 years after the end of the second world war, continue to treat roma as second-class citizens is not acceptable.

for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. many of us here today want the baltic sea to be a healthy sea again. you're supposed to be able to eat cod that's been caught close by without having to worry about it disappearing. major steps have already been taken to make this a reality. after a long struggle, european politicians agreed last year that we must stop overfishing. all of us who have been involved in the fight for the eu's new fisheries law, which says that you cannot fish more than the sea can handle, are proud and want to see political action in the same direction. we're on the right track, but there's still a lot of work to do. the baltic cod is not doing well. after a few years of positive development, the number of cod is now decreasing again. the ones that do are small and skinny. this trend can be reversed if all the countries around our fragile inland sea cooperate. by reducing fishing for a number of years, we give cod a chance to recover. it is important to take a holistic approach that takes into account the interaction of all species in the sea, so that, for example, the larger predatory fish are not deprived of their food. the eu is now due to adopt a management plan for cod, haddock and plaice in the baltic sea. for us, it is clear that the plan will lead to sustainable fishing and a long-term good marine environment. we have therefore put forward proposals to keep catches of fish at levels which will allow fish stocks to grow. but not all our colleagues in the european parliament and the council of ministers agree. there are strong forces that are working against sustainable fishing. the baltic sea plan will serve as a model for future management plans in different eu sea basins. which is why it's especially important that we get it right this time.
member of the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is a pleasure for me to take part in this debate on indirect land use change, or iluc, as it is known in english. we are faced with a possible agreement that mitigates iluc and at the same time protects the existing industry. moreover, it also shows a clear path for advanced biofuels. this is a significant achievement, and i'm aware that it hasn't been easy. i hope you will support this agreement and that we can conclude the negotiations. i would like to express my sincere thanks to all the honourable members, including those who worked on this dossier with such conviction during the last term, and i am especially grateful to mr torvalds and his negotiating team and to mr la via for their efforts to get the dossier through to this final stage. the compromise package is the result of very close negotiations. i also thank you for the excellent cooperation and for giving the commission many opportunities to share and explain its views. the compromise package before us today is much stronger than the council's position at first reading thanks to the determination of this parliament. let me say a few words about the key elements of the compromise package. i will start with the limit on the contribution that biofuels produced from certain crops can make to the objectives of the renewable energy directive. this is known as the 'food crop biofuel ceiling'. the commission had originally proposed that this ceiling should be 5%, i.e. half of the target for renewable energy in transport. this level was chosen to protect existing production levels and thus mitigate the impact of the iluc while protecting the existing industry. the level in the undertaking package is set at 7%. it is higher than the european parliament initially proposed, but could still mitigate the iluc. in addition, other elements have been added to strengthen this roof. the commitment package seeks to foster the transition to advanced biofuels, which is very important for our future, as set out in the communication on an energy union. member states shall set an advanced biofuels target with a reference value of 0,5%. it is true that it is not a binding target, but it is also true that member states have a tight deadline to be explicit and define their level of policy ambition in relation to advanced biofuels, and this provides a clear signal to the industry, while still being realistic for member states. i hope that, together with existing initiatives such as the ner300 programme and horizon 2020, it will help to continue the development of this industry in the european union. in addition, the commitment package will improve the greenhouse gas performance of biofuels by raising the greenhouse gas savings threshold for new installations, which must operate at a savings of 60% compared to fossil fuels. the commitment package also requires fuel suppliers and the commission to report estimated iluc emissions, indicators that are based on the best available scientific information, which will improve greenhouse gas reporting and increase transparency. and the compromise package also removes an element of the council's position which the commission and parliament opposed. i am referring to the extension of the multiple accounting for advanced biofuels to the overall objectives of the renewable energy directive. this would have weakened the 2020 package, and its removal is a significant achievement. the commission shall, by means of implementing acts, lay down the criteria for the selection of the most appropriate biofuels for the purpose of the specific objectives of the union's biogas policy. the current biofuel industry is protected, while the developing advanced biofuel industry is given encouraging signals for its future. i know that not everything that was in the european parliament's first reading position is reflected in this proposal for a final agreement, but given the difficulties of this complex dossier, i think that parliament has achieved very important achievements.
for the ppe group. mr president i would also like to thank mr torvalds for the excellent work he has done on this report. i remember when i was new to this european parliament almost 11 years ago. at the time, many said that the renewable energy targets were too low. it was said that the eu was doing too little and too slowly to promote ethanol in particular. ethanol was then described as the way to heaven, and in sweden the environmental party/social democrats decided that every petrol station would have to have a special petrol pump with which to fill up with ethanol. but that was then, because now there's another sound in the groove. today, ethanol is described more as the road to hell, and in working on this report it is clear that the greens in particular, but also the socialist group, have done everything possible to water down the eu's renewable fuel targets. ethanol, it's going away, and the sooner the better. when i get home, those gas pumps are gonna be rusting. but biofuels are neither heaven nor hell, and we don't have to choose between food and fuel. you can do both. biofuels are a necessary part, i would say, of a successful climate policy, particularly in all those countries, such as sweden, where we have great need for transport. but one thing is certain, we need stable and long-term rules of the game for renewable fuels, and that is what would be at risk if this legislative report had gone wrong. so i am glad that we were able to stop the demands coming from large sections of this house, particularly from colleagues on the left and in the green group, to phase out this target and to create hard limits on the possibility of creating new advanced biofuels. this would have made it very difficult to make biofuels from the forest, among other things, but i think if we succeed in this, we have created a stable set of rules. i therefore hope that all members, especially those who dare to call themselves green, will vote in favour of the new rules, because if you do not, you are really only supporting fossil fuels.
blue-card question.– you said that we are focusing too much on biofuels in the european union. would you agree that this is the fault of the greens and their actions 10 years ago? secondly, you said that agri-biofuels are not delivering. are you aware of the fact that we have ethanol produced in europe that is third-party certified as having 90% co2 reductions? are you willing to agree that there are differences between different first-generation biofuels? thirdly, if parliament were to vote, as you will vote today, against this legislation, and if you had eur 1 billion, would you dare to invest that in the production of second-generation advanced biofuels if parliament decided, as you want, to kill the rules and definitions of how they should be produced?
member of the commission. - madam president, i should first like to thank the rapporteur, mr faria, for his constructive approach to the commission's proposal and for all his work in the negotiations with the council with a view to reaching a rapid agreement at second reading. i am also grateful for the work of the previous rapporteur, mr skylakakis, and of the other rapporteurs in the shadow of this dossier. the regulation on the monitoring, reporting and verification of co2 emissions from maritime transport is consistent with the 2030 energy and climate policy framework. shipping should not be the only economic sector not regulated in this area and should make its contribution to the fight against climate change. the introduction of transparency, based on reliable and robust data, will help to mobilise the sector's exceptional remaining potential to reduce its emissions at very low or no cost. and this means that the additional costs of monitoring, reporting and verification will undoubtedly be covered by the subsequent savings in terms of fuel consumption. i should stress that the european union's primary interest is to find global solutions to maritime emissions and that the european union will therefore continue to drive progress in the discussions within the international maritime organization. in this regard, the monitoring, reporting and verification regulation sends an important political signal ahead of the next imo committee meeting to discuss global data collection in london in may next year and also ahead of the united nations climate change conference in paris in december 2015. the commission welcomes the text presented by the council, which reflects the agreement reached in the tripartite dialogues, and which strikes a delicate balance between collecting the most relevant data for the sector and imposing the least possible administrative burden. on the one hand, the approach is kept rational by covering only co2 emissions, but on the other hand and in line with international discussions, the monitoring covers energy efficiency parameters which have incorporated important improvements agreed by the council and the european parliament. increased transparency on energy efficiency in the sector should enable the adoption of emission mitigation measures by the sector and, in addition, this regulation ensures that, in the event of an agreement on a legal framework for a future measure applicable at global level, we can adapt our monitoring system. finally, the proposed agreement - as i have already said - is a very important signal from the european union in preparation for the paris conference, indicating that we are committed to action to combat climate change and at the same time promoting further progress at international level in the case of maritime emissions, mainly within the international maritime organisation.
for the ppe group. president. - the debate is closed. shipping is the lifeblood of world trade. more than ninety percent of world trade is transported by sea. shipping is not only cost-effective, it is also, and above all, environmentally efficient, because despite accounting for such a large share of transport, it accounts for just under three per cent of global co2 emissions. nevertheless, shipping must play its part in taking climate responsibility, and i am therefore pleased that we are now taking the first step in bringing shipping into eu climate policy. i hope that this will lead to us speeding up the process under way in the international maritime organisation so that the rest of the world can follow our example. shipping needs global rules to function because it is a global industry. for me, who negotiated this on behalf of my group, it has been particularly important to ensure that the new maritime rules are cost-effective and do not reduce the competitiveness of shipping. every new burden we impose on shipping risks making the use of transport less efficient, moving things to, say, trucks, and this is particularly serious if we expect the rest of the world to follow our example. so i'm a little extra proud of two victories that i feel i've pushed for and earned in this work. the first is that we will focus on co2 and the proposal we are adopting today focuses on co2 which is the most important and most serious greenhouse gas in this context. the second is that we focus on the larger vessels, and we exclude the smaller vessels, which account for 40 percent of our fleet but only discharge 10 percent. it is crucial to make this cost-effective, and we have heard that. now sweden and other countries are pushing for the imo so that we can also bring the rest of the world with us, because all the ships in the world must take the same responsibility for the climate as european ships do after this decision.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the principle of non-discrimination on grounds of pregnancy and maternity leave has a long history in the eu. it was established in 1958. since the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon, the recognition of the principle of non-discrimination on grounds of pregnancy and maternity leave has been further extended. despite this, discrimination against women is still very much present, both in the workplace and at home, which provides an incentive for discussing maternity leave from a broader perspective. today, the gender pay gap for the same job is 16 percent, and the gender pension gap is 39 percent. if we look at the eu27, 19 percent of all women live in poverty, and 34 percent of all single mothers of working age are at risk. in addition, women are very overrepresented in temporary and parttime employment. the situation in the eu today is forcing women to return home to do unpaid work such as child rearing and caring for relatives. european women need better, legally binding protection against discrimination on the grounds of pregnancy and maternity leave. for me this is a matter of solidarity with our sisters in europe who are now suffering setbacks in terms of economic self-determination and emancipation and participation in the labour market because of their possible pregnancy.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the eu's accession to the convention on the rights of persons with disabilities is a milestone in the human rights field. it is also very important that parliament takes an active part in the review of the implementation of the convention. that is why the resolution we are going to vote on tomorrow is of great importance. the eu clearly has a long way to go to ensure that people with disabilities can live a life on an equal footing with other people in the eu. the failure of the council to adopt a position on the 2008 commission's anti-discrimination directive effectively denies protection against discrimination against people with disabilities and other persons in vulnerable situations. i would like to make two points regarding the implementation of the convention in the eu. the first is that we must see people with disabilities as individuals of different backgrounds, cultures, genders, beliefs and ethnicities. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we pay attention to the fact that people with disabilities may also be subject to other forms of discrimination. we also need to recognise that people with disabilities have different needs in order to live an active and independent life in our society. it is not reasonable for a disability to limit a person in what others take for granted, such as where they choose to live, what education they want, where they want to work, what they want to do in their free time, what goods and services they want to use. the eu can play an important role in this by developing guidelines. however, the starting point must always be that the obstacle is in the environment and not in man.

‒ this year's sakharov prize laureate, denis mukwege, has reminded us of the enormous impact of the export of conflict minerals on the people of his country. as the problem is particularly acute in the democratic republic of congo and the great lakes region, i have been campaigning for legislation focusing on this area as the shadow rapporteur for the epp group in the committee on foreign affairs. the epp's line is one of mandatory legislation for refiners and importers. virtually all minerals purchased by eu companies pass through these channels, and focusing on these links in the supply chain is a timely measure that lays the foundation for companies to exercise due diligence while safeguarding jobs and growth. this, together with active eu support for regional initiatives for conflict-free mineral extraction, strengthens the conditions for legitimate activities and for breaking the link between mineral extraction and the financing of armed groups. i also support this position because there are several legislative proposals that have been shelved because of disagreements between parliament and the council. almost all member states support the commission's approach to voluntary self-certification. the european parliament's taking a tougher stance than the commission is good, but a constructive negotiation with the member states is crucial for legislation on conflict minerals to become a reality.

blue-card answer.– thank you very much. i would like to say that there has been, just in the past week, progress in confidence-building measures on resolving the conflict in cyprus. i do not think that using the fact that you are a member of the european parliament constantly just to harass and attack turkey is constructive for a long-term, fair, win-win solution for all the people of cyprus.
author.– madam president, the crisis has caused youth unemployment to reach alarming levels in many member states and regions. many young people are deprived of taking on a first job and are thus obstructed from assuming independent adult lives. in 2013, to urgently help address the situation, the youth employment initiative was adopted. the purpose of this initiative is to provide funding to promote youth employment in the most affected regions of the european union, including by establishing youth guarantee schemes. with a view to ensuring swift implementation, the youth employment initiative was front-loaded and expenditure was made eligible as of 1 september 2013. yet well over one year into the programme, the results on the ground do not meet initial expectations. measures designed to allow for a quick uptake of funds have not led to a quick and efficient mobilisation of the youth employment initiative, and opportunities to urgently address youth unemployment have been left unused. the initiative was initially proposed by the european council, yet at present – over one-and-a-half years after expenditure was made eligible – member states’ use of its resources falls far short of expectations, and progress is uneven. besides a lack of liquidity to launch the youth employment initiative, an issue now addressed by the proposed substantial increase in pre-financing, what is holding member states back? how does the council intend to urgently encourage swift and efficient implementation? the member states faced with the highest youth unemployment rates and hardest hit by the crisis are, on average, lagging behind in using youth employment initiative resources and implementing youth guarantee schemes, while long-term policies are needed to address this issue. what specific hurdles do they face? what improvements can the council promise? can the council ensure sound funding for the youth employment initiative once the frontloading funds have been used? parliament is expecting a quick, efficient and result-oriented use of the additional pre-financing within 12 months after the entry into force of the amending regulation. what measures does the council envisage in order to assure the young unemployed, and parliament, of this swift use of funds? how does it intend to report on progress to the young unemployed, to parliament, and to other stakeholders involved? can the council promise an assessment of progress six months after the entry into force of the amending regulation? how does the council intend to proceed where those results do not meet expectations, even after the current increased pre-financing?
mr president, i would like to thank you. we must remember that unemployment is not simply due to a lack of work or a lack of education. for a large group of people in the eu, unemployment is primarily about racism and discrimination. roma have always been among the most marginalised groups in the eu. in the bad times that have been, of course, it is much harder. unemployment among roma is three times higher than among the majority population. roma youth are overrepresented in the group of young people not working or studying in the eu. the member states must therefore make special efforts to support roma youth in their youth guarantees, and eu youth initiatives must also be specifically targeted at roma. this is a crucial issue if europe's young roma are to have a chance at a better life than their parents and if we are to put an end to the anti-gypsyism that exists. i hope you take it with you.
‒ i have voted against all three reports on the common defence and security policy. they are all militaristic, call for a major rearmament and emphasise the link with nato. in addition, the athena mechanism is to be strengthened to finance eu military missions, and it is argued that eu combat teams should be deployed in all types of crisis management. the eu has been described as a peace project, but this is not reflected in the reports in question, which are instead characterised by the desire to build a military force for a federal eu. as an advocate of détente and peace, i want the eu and its member states to emphasise civil peaceful strategies for conflict resolution instead. that is why i have voted against these three reports.

mr president, i would like to thank you. as we debate in parliament today the development aid to eritrea and highlight the human rights violations of the regime, i would like to remind you of a figure: 5 000. on tuesday 2 june, it will be 5,000 days since the arrest and imprisonment of the eu citizen dawit isaak in eritrea. since 2001, this swedish-eritrean journalist has been detained in the most appalling conditions without trial, without contact with the outside world. mr president, this is one of the most serious human rights violations to date involving sweden, and it is high time to increase the pressure on the eritrean dictatorship. the uk and canada have taken measures to prevent eritrean embassies in the west from enforcing the regime's forced taxation of citizens in exile. joint action by the eu member states in this regard could lead to a significant cut in the flow of money to the dictatorship in asmara. it would also be in response to a direct call from the un security council. let us therefore work within the eu to stop the illegal tax collection by the eritrean regime. let us work together to extend and strengthen sanctions against the dictatorship and let us link the development aid we are discussing today with clearer demands for respect for human rights. 5,000 long days have passed since dawit isaak was imprisoned. eritrea's prisons are full of other innocents, of other prisoners of conscience. the eu must now respond with firm demands and tougher measures against the regime in asmara.
