author.– madam president, around the world, including in europe, 28 girls every minute are forced to marry. they are exposed to abuse and violence and are denied their rights to health and education. they are robbed of their childhood. the un special representative on sexual violence in conflict, zainab hawa bangura, has denounced horrible sexual abuses and organised rapes committed by daesh/isis. young girls are sold naked at the market and promised as brides at a price after recruitment. we also see an increased number of children being given in marriage in refugee camps, as desperate parents try to find a way out of poverty. i want to mention the heart-breaking story of heda, a young girl from chechnya forced to marry a man three times her age, despite her clear opposition. the life of the journalist who reported this is now under threat. just last week an international conference on child marriage was organised by the ngo girls not brides. it emerged that there are camps in countries like zambia and mozambique to initiate girls in sex and prepare them for marriage, sometimes before they turn nine. these are just a few chilling stories, but there are thousands more and none of us can remain unmoved by them. child marriage is not only a violation of children’s rights but also a form of violence against women. unfortunately, only 11 member states have ratified the council of europe’s istanbul convention on combating violence against women that recognises child marriage as a criminal offence. i therefore call on all the member states to ratify the convention and on the commission actively to encourage more member states to enforce it. we cannot close our eyes to this horrific practice. it is time to act in order to end it. we are talking about children. i am proud that the epp group took the initiative in this public debate in plenary and i warmly thank the committee on women’s rights and its chair for this report. let us join forces across political groups and at all levels to put an end to child marriage. the consequences are devastating: girls’ health is at stake, with serious risk of complications in pregnancy and childbirth, and even hiv infections; and girls are exposed to sexual abuse, domestic violence and even honour killing. it is also a question of empowerment. girls forced to marry are forced out of school, and education offers the best chance for girls to develop skills and have a role in society. keeping girls out of school is a way to condemn them to poverty and discrimination. i welcome the fact that the european external action service and the member states have made child marriage a priority in the eu action plan for human rights and democracy and the eu development cooperation gender action plan. however, progress has been slow and uneven. i therefore call on the vice-president of the commission/ high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy to implement this commitment in all the dialogues and programmes with third countries and regional organisations. the upcoming international conference on financing for development, in addis ababa in july 2015, will be a test for our leaders. child marriage should be included as a priority in the post-2015 development agenda. we need to work with law enforcement authorities, the judicial system and the police, and also to provide training and technical assistance to promote the adoption and enforcement of legislation prohibiting early and forced marriages, including a minimum age for marriage. however, this is not enough. the eu also has an important role in supporting information and awareness campaigns, and our challenge is to combat stereotypes. violence against children and women should never be justified by culture, tradition or religion, and it is time to break the vicious circle of poverty, submission and violence. we need to work together with religious communities, schools and the media to change attitudes. we need to help families and community leaders to keep girls in school and protect them from violence. let me thank the many international organisations, including unicef, and the many ngos and civil society bodies who are doing a great job. this is about girls’ dignity, and our message to the 50 million girls a year should be: ‘you are not alone.’ the european parliament will remain committed to work with all concerned to keep at the top of the eu agenda a zero tolerance policy against child marriage.
mr president, i would like to thank you. marriages in which one or both partners are children should never be recognized as legal. child marriage is, by definition, forced marriage. unfortunately, there are those who - we are talking about africa, we are talking about third countries - even within our borders. the most surprising thing is that we are sadly aware of this, but we are not ensuring that there is protection for all these girls and children who are subjected to forced marriage. the istanbul convention has committed itself to working actively against male violence against women. child marriage is a form of structural violence against women that perpetuates inequalities between men and women in society. the commission is also taking an important step in the fight against child marriage by setting out clear and concrete guidelines on how states can start acting now to stop child marriage and other forms of male violence against women. the istanbul convention is a convention that all member states should ratify. the commission is invited to launch a process for eu accession to the convention and to develop financial support for the ngos working against certain groups on these issues to raise awareness.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. first of all, i would like to thank rapporteur noichl and the other colleagues on the committee on equality for working together. for us in gue/ngl, gender equality and feminism are absolutely central to the societies we want to build and live in. it's fundamental to more just societies, to a future that values equality, that sees that our democracies today are not democratic enough, and that sees that our economies must integrate both production and reproduction. it takes quite a change to end hundreds, if not thousands, of years of patriarchal rule. so the dream of a society where it's equal is a big dream, and women's struggles for justice and equality have been marred by hardship and resistance - everything from the right to vote, the right to work, the right to inherit, the right to abortion and contraception. i'm proud and grateful for the sisters who went before me, before us who are sitting here tonight. but they will also be our benchmark, and it's amazing how much resistance there is actually left. those of us who work on these issues don't always share the same feminist strategies, but most of us are actually genuinely interested in building societies that are more equal, where girls and boys, women and men have the same opportunities and rights, and that we're stepping into the future. it's about the struggle for economic independence, the struggle for a life that is not limited by violence and the threat of violence, a society where we expand the space of life for girls and women, where girls are raised in the firm belief that the right to their own bodies and their own sexuality is theirs, not just on paper in a legal paragraph, but in real life, in real life. young women don't want to live in the europe of old men. they want a parliament here that takes action and it is a long time since the eu actually did anything to create a more equal society. legislation for a few hundred women on company boards is not really enough. this report, which we are voting on tomorrow, is not perfect but it is ambitious, as some colleagues have said. it takes a holistic approach and it has been supported and soaked in the equality committee, and civil society has been given a say. this is not about subsidiarity, this is about whether you stand up for women's rights and equality or whether you continue to be part of the old conservative resistance. tomorrow we will vote against the epp resolution and we will vote for the rapporteur's resolution from the committee on gender equality. blue-card answer.– in an equal society i would not dream of calling anyone anything, but in this society i have been fighting for women’s and girls’ equal rights for long enough, and i have met a lot of resistance. believe me: that resistance is mainly from older men, and in this chamber that is even more true. so i am not taking it anymore; i am not having it anymore. i can name something for what it is: we see a lot of male resistance to gender equality in this room, and it has to stop. i want equality in this chamber, too, by the way – quotas tomorrow!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have given birth to three children and am not disabled to sit in the european parliament. the overall wage gap in the eu is currently 16.4% women earn 16.4% less than men despite having the same job and performing the same tasks. this injustice is due to a number of factors. it is because men have almost always occupied decision-making positions and thus controlled pay. it is because we women have long been seen as a mere means of reproduction, which makes the female worker much more unreliable than her male colleague because she is expected to bear children. the difference in treatment between men and women is not just a problem for the moment - it also poses enormous problems for the future, such as the eu pension gap now at 39%, which puts women in an unsustainable situation for life. women make up more than half of europe's population, but only 18.6% of eu board members are women and only 5% of eu board chairpersons are women. it is therefore time that we, as a first step, seriously consider what measures are needed to ensure that women have the same opportunities as men to lead companies and enterprises. at the same time, we must also dare to break the norm that values men more highly in terms of pay. and if it's not obvious to you already how important this is, just equal participation in the labor market would lead to a gdp per capita increase of 12.4 percent by 2030. what i have just briefly discussed are just a few examples of the injustices that women face daily. injustices that must be put to an end, and maria noichl's report is a good tool for that. if we do not vote for her report and the report of the femm committee, women's economic independence and emancipation will be put on hold again, as has happened so many times before.

‒ today the european parliament voted on a report on the eu strategy for equality between women and men after 2015. the report contains many urgent proposals for action against, for example, violence by men against women, discrimination and the gender pay gap. but the report also contains proposals which i cannot support, for reasons of subsidiarity, among others. this is the case, for example, with compulsory quotas and family policy. as regards the literature on sexual and reproductive health, i maintain my view that the eu should respect the responsibility for health policy at national level. in substance, the christian democrats believe that there are two individuals worth protecting in a pregnancy and that it is therefore in the interest of society to reduce the number of abortions, but that the woman has the right to make the decisive decision on abortion during the first part of the pregnancy. the christian democrats believe that the state and the legislature cannot remain neutral in relation to the ethical dimension of the abortion issue. this report does not mention at all that the fetus also has a protective value. i voted, after a comprehensive assessment of the content of the report, no.
in writing. ‒ i support the eu's long-term and forward-looking gender equality strategy and i voted in favour of the resolution in the final vote. i voted against certain parts, however, which dealt with, for example, quotas and parental insurance, as i do not believe that the eu should decide in these areas. i supported other part-votes, for example on the right to abortion, but i would like to stress that this is not a legislative area at eu level.
‒ we moderates voted for the main proposals in the vote on gender equality. we are in favour of a gender equality strategy at eu level in matters where europe decides jointly. it is at eu level that we can best combat trafficking, among other things. we are opposed to the left-wing rhetoric and superficial economic theories contained in the report, which directly contradict its purpose. it is spending and deficit spending that has depleted the money that welfare needs and undermined jobs and employment. the report in many cases lacks respect for the member states and the individual's right to self-determination, which risks undermining swedish legislation on family policy, lgbtq rights and abortion rights, among other issues. these are matters that belong to the member states, and we moderates do not want to bring them up at eu level, which would as with all other eu matters force compromises with other countries and values other than those that characterize current swedish legislation.

‒ i have voted against the report. the main reason is that the report is against fair world trade, because it requires poor countries in the global south to meet the high standards of intellectual property protection, or else they are denied market access. it is a trade strategy that looks only at the interests of big business in the eu, and not at the need for development and fair trade. nor has the report made any distinction between developing and least developed countries, which is a blow to the least developed countries in terms of opportunities for favourable and developing trade. the report also proposes the creation of a new eu body for these issues, which i oppose. that is the main reason why i voted no.
in writing. - i voted against this report because it is going in the wrong direction. on the part of the greens, we have put forward drafting proposals on the need to balance different rights and stressed the importance of proportionality and legal certainty. we have also included some of this in the text, but the report as a whole is problematic. in particular, it pushes for a system that violates the principle of messenger neutrality and where isps are required to monitor private individuals' mail and internet traffic.
‒ i have voted against the report. the report seeks to strengthen intellectual property rights in a way that further increases the control of the large companies in this area. the report argues, among other things, for the right of artists to protect their works. i support that too, but i don't think copyright is going to get any better if you strengthen the intellectual property rights of big companies. the report also expresses a very positive opinion on the work of the european observatory, despite criticism from many citizens and ngos for its one-sided focus on protecting the interests of companies. the fact that the report has beautiful phrases on fundamental rights is a positive thing, but it does not change the whole picture. that is why i am voting no.

madam president, i think that it is important to state that this discussion has arisen very much from the fact that russia is using its energy resources for political and strategic purposes against member states of the european union, as well as others. this is a threat to peace and stability, and also to the sovereignty of our societies. we need to diversify, we need to develop new energy sources, and we need to phase out those energy sources that have the highest levels of carbon dioxide emissions. this is very compatible with the climate strategy of the european union, because when we reduce fossil fuels we are also reducing our dependence on russia and on other regimes that use their petrol resources in order to have a political impact. this leads me to an important conclusion for this chamber: the green’s policies are leading us to the increased use of black fossil fuels. we see it in a number of countries: when phasing out nuclear power instead of phasing out fossil fuels, we are increasing our dependence on countries like russia and we are increasing our dependence on fossil fuels. let us do it the other way around: reduce fossil fuels and reduce our dependence on the countries we do not want to be dependent on.

mr president, it is about time that we talked about the 2 million people that are fleeing at the moment in europe. it is an exodus of the magnitude of the bosnian war. there are 1.2 million internally-displaced people in ukraine: children, women, men – innocent people. there are 800 000 refugees leaving ukraine. there are humanitarian convoys from the united nations, from the european union, that do not get through to the people in need. there are 3.2 million people in need of urgent help, according to the european union humanitarian office. why is no one talking about them? they are victims who are kidnapped and hijacked. but there is this fear of confronting russia, which wants to subvert the order of peace and security in europe by using force and, more and more, heavy weapons. i am glad that this parliament, with a strong majority, has supported the resolution that simply tells a member of the security council to respect the rule of law and international law.
- i've put my voice down. the report's criticism of russia is correct. i condemn the annexation of crimea, as well as russia's human rights violations such as the persecution of lgbtq people and restrictions on freedom of expression and assembly. my criticism of putin's authoritarian and corrupt rule is crystal clear. but i strongly disagree with the report's conclusion that there is a need to consolidate and deepen the eu's common foreign and security policy. on the contrary, i believe that we need non-aligned eu countries, such as sweden, which retain their own independent capacity to act for détente, peace and disarmament. i am also opposed to the closer cooperation between the eu and nato. militarisation and the building of great powers do not solve conflicts. i would also have liked the report to have put more emphasis on, and focused on, strategies for peaceful civil conflict resolution. that is why i choose to abstain.
in writing. we swedish social democrats think it is good to analyse and respond appropriately to biased russian information disseminated in various eu languages, but we do not think that the commission should allocate money to do this independently. the commission should instead develop projects to monitor the spread of russian propaganda within and outside the eu in cooperation with the national authorities of the member states. we therefore decided not to support all the points in paragraph 12 of the report.
in writing. we voted in favour of the saudargas report on european energy security. some of the wording used is very broad and thus includes both positive and negative elements, which can make it difficult to draw the line correctly between a technical statement and a value-added message. there is also a difference between national policies, where we ourselves decide on our energy mix, and a european perspective, where it can be stated that nuclear power, for example, will be used for a long time to come. we therefore wish to point out that our intention to refer to low-carbon technologies, a low-emission society, etc. does not in any way represent a departure from our line on nuclear energy, which will continue to follow the result of the referendum in accordance with the decisions of the social democratic congress. we are pursuing a development, both at swedish and european level, with energy efficiency and renewable energy sources in focus and where the highest environmental and safety requirements are to be set throughout the production chain and emissions are to be costly.
in writing. - i've turned my voice down. the turkish people must decide for themselves whether they want to be part of the eu or not. but before membership can be considered, the turkish government must demonstrate clearly that it respects democracy, human rights and international law. the key issues include the safeguarding of the democratic rights of the kurds and all other ethnic minorities, the freedom of the political opposition to work without risk of reprisals, the development of gender equality policies and respect for the rights of all women, the active fight against widespread corruption and the end of the occupation of parts of cyprus. once these fundamental issues are resolved, there are no obstacles to turkey's membership of the eu, but the decision must be taken in a democratic referendum where the people of turkey themselves decide. on this basis of principle, i choose to abstain.
the european parliament has adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia, calling on the eu to take action to combat the situation in the former yugoslavia. we consider turkey to be one of the eu's most important partners in a number of key areas, such as energy and trade, counter-terrorism and security, migration and refugee reception. we should therefore do everything we can to speed up the reform process, and the most effective way to do that is through these negotiations. we therefore particularly welcome the fact that parliament is calling on the member states to open chapters 23 (justice and fundamental rights) and 24 (freedom, security and justice) and thereby to increase the pace of reform in these areas. we also look with confidence to the results of this weekend's elections, which show that turkish democracy is viable.
mr president, i would like to thank you. major sporting events are supposed to be a neutral venue where sport and athletic performance are the focus. unfortunately, this is jeopardised by the fact that the competitions are held in countries with dubious democracies, where the games are transformed into a political tool for the regime to legitimise human rights violations. the council and the high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy should clearly call on the european olympic committee to demand that the host countries guarantee respect for human rights. the european union is now sending a clear signal on this issue - not least in support of the forces fighting for human rights in the country - when the european olympic committee fails to take its responsibility for this before the baku games. one problem that has been found at all major sporting events, regardless of the host country, is the huge increase in demand for sexual services. major sports event organisers need to clearly condemn prostitution and take greater responsibility to counter an increase in prostitution as a result of such events. to combat the exploitation of people, in a variety of areas, from social upheavals to working conditions. these are key criteria for the sustainability and future development of these sports events. i think the eu has a great opportunity to put pressure on this. the same is true of the case mentioned, with leyla yunus, for example, and others like her. they had leyla yunus as a finalist for our sakharov prize solely because of her work for human rights and the values that she stood for. the values and the work that we valued in even being able to honor her, that's what she's been imprisoned for. i think the eu as an institution must put pressure on and make use of the expert group set up in may 2011. there are ethical and moral rules that we can push around, this is not worthy of the european union, not to act.
mr president, i would like to thank you. daesh has carried out systematic attacks on a large number of archaeological, religious and cultural sites in syria and iraq. now we can only hope that they will not also rob the world of the irreplaceable cultural heritage that is the city of palmyra. this recurrent strategy has been described as cultural cleansing. in particular, when the acts are directed against members of a religious or ethnic group in order to prevent the practice, remembrance and learning of religion, they may amount to persecution and, in certain circumstances, to genocide. but the resolution also deals with human rights. the imprisonment of syrian human rights defender mazen darwish and other freedom fighters is yet another testimony to the gross violations of human rights that occur every day in syria. i express my admiration and solidarity with all syrian activists who continue their struggle in a conflict-torn environment and i call for the immediate release of all those who have been the victims of arbitrary detention.
in writing. the developments in the black sea basin are very worrying and we support the european council's condemnation of russia's annexation of crimea. we are very concerned about the reports of war crimes and human rights violations in the area and believe it is important that the eu should speak out against this with a clear voice. however, we do not believe that a unilateral focus on military build-up in the region is the best way forward. we would have liked the report to also highlight other appropriate measures to deal with the situation in the black sea basin in a more comprehensive way.
in writing. – in light of the escalation in the conflict between russia and the western democracies, including inter alia the build-up of military forces, the risk of a possible arms race and russian threats of use of nuclear arms, i consider the calls for nato forces to be moved to the black sea basin an ill-advised and confrontational suggestion. because of this, i abstained in the vote on this resolution.
in writing. ‒ we fully share the criticism of fifa and welcome the legal process currently under way. it is good that the swedish football association has not only distanced itself from the corruption in fifa but is working hard and longterm to counteract irregularities in football. but it is not for the european parliament to control in detail how the associations in the member states are to organise themselves. this applies regardless of the size of the association. we are assuming that if investigations reveal that russia or qatar have been awarded the hosting rights after bribery and other corruption, fifa will take action and move the tournament. and there may be reason to consider whether sweden's national team will go to the world cup in russia and reason to criticize the working conditions at the world cup buildings in qatar. but it is ultimately up to the individual football associations to decide whether to participate or not. it is not for the eu to decide. indeed, if members of the european parliament wish to form opinions on these issues, they can do so without using the legislative instruments of the european parliament as a platform.

mr president, having listened to this debate, it is striking that so very many in this chamber believe that you can solve the problem of a deficit crisis and the debt crisis by increasing the deficits and the debts. if we look around, in reality it is very easy to see that, where you have allowed the deficits to increase, you have undermined employment, growth and investment. this is the true reality, and still some here believe that you have to finance investments with the money you do not have, instead of having budgets applied using the money you do have. i say this because the rules we possess have value and credibility if we follow them, and if we follow them we will allow for the investments and the growth we need. that is why this report is good: it means that we can follow the rules that we have agreed upon. that makes the european union strong.
madam president, i would like to thank you. as i see it, the european commission's action plan on corporate taxation is both good and bad. i think it is good that the proposal for a common consolidated corporate tax base is now being relaunched in a pragmatic way. the gradual process envisaged by the commission is probably the only realistic way forward. i also think it is good that the commission is making a proper argument about how the oecd-led beps initiative should be implemented at eu level. this calls for a well-thought-out strategy. i also think it is good that the commission is establishing a blacklist of tax havens. the list can be a useful tool in the fight against tax evasion. i think it is a bad thing that the commission is burying the requirement of transparency in a vague consultation. instead, the commission should have made a bold proposal to extend the country-by-country reporting requirements for large crossborder companies to all sectors. anyone who follows this matter knows very well that the knowledge base for such a proposal is already quite sufficient.
‒ the efsi enjoys very broad support both among the member states and in the european parliament. the proposal has been put in the right direction in parliament on crucial points: reducing the depletion of european research funding, ensuring that member states' contributions are fully covered by the budgetary rules of the stability pact and that the efsi cannot be used as an excuse for not implementing the necessary structural reforms. in the negotiations with the council of ministers, the european parliament has unfortunately had to give way to several of these important demands. it is noteworthy that the swedish government in the council of ministers has supported taking money from research, shifting it to new money and clarifying the budgetary rules of the stability pact. the efsi does not solve the fundamental problem of declining investment levels in europe. europe has gradually become too overregulated, too slow and too expensive to invest in. to make europe more attractive for investment, reforms are needed to boost competitiveness and reduce fiscal deficits. it is now crucial not to lose sight of what is really needed to increase the willingness to invest in europe. countries in dire need of reform cannot rely on money from brussels to get them out of the crisis and therefore fail to implement the necessary reforms. in such cases, the lower investment levels risk becoming permanent.
in writing. - i voted against the report. the idea of an investment plan for europe is a good one, but the proposal at issue is not a real investment plan, since it is not really a question of real investment with real money. a central part of the proposal at issue is the desire for large-scale private investment, where the main motive will be profit-making rather than fighting unemployment and growing social inequalities within the eu. we know that there is a huge need for investment across the eu, for example to strengthen infrastructure, develop renewable energy, improve the public sector and welfare. massive and real green investment in these sectors would boost the economy, create lots of new jobs and help solve many of the structural and social problems facing eu countries today. however, this requires a new approach from the eu, it requires abandoning the market-liberal approach of austerity and recognising that what is needed above all is massive public green investment. i would support such an offensive investment plan, but the proposal at hand is something quite different. that is why i am voting no.

‒ today we voted on financial contributions from the so-called globalisation fund. the fund is designed to help people who have lost their jobs as a result of the closure of european companies. i am consistently voting no to disbursements from this fund for two reasons: first, labour market policies and financial support for the unemployed should be decided on, and financed, at national level not at eu level. secondly, the fund's basic idea is that globalisation is something negative - which is not true. globalization and free trade lead to growth and peace.
in writing. we swedish social democrats note that there is a need to develop economic governance within the euro area. as long as the process does not damage the internal market or the interests of non-euro countries, we have no objection in general to the fact that a renewed reform of the euro area has now been launched. the report contains significant ambiguities on a number of specific points. this includes the way in which the 'convergence guidelines' would work, how the esm and the fiscal compact would be 'integrated into the community framework', how a 'european financial authority' would be constructed and how a 'fiscal policy capacity' for the euro area would relate to the eu budget. the uncertainties have made it difficult to take a definitive position on these points.
mr president, i think this document by mr balz and mr gualtieri is a very good one. it is extremely important that we get deeper capital markets in europe, but i always get a little sceptical when people say that we are to launch a capital markets union. i would like to disclose to this plenary that we already have a capital markets union: it is called the european union, and its aim is for an internal market and the free flow of goods, capital services and people. i say this because this discussion is very much about the shortcomings of the past, where we have not been able to get rid of the hindrances, barriers and fragmentation. we do have well-functioning capital markets in the union, here and there, which are rather deep and which are providing the capital. the problem is not the amount of liquidity but the amount of profitable investments. there are some things we need to do in order to improve the capital markets of the european union. first of all, we should skip the idea of the financial transaction tax. secondly, we need to have a review of all the legislation we have dealt with so far: some good, some maybe not so good, and some which hinders. we need to ensure that we are not regulating in a way that creates hindrances and that we are opening up for the free flow of capital – that is the way to get more investments.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am positive about the construction of a capital markets union. it would be valuable to reduce the companies' dependence on banks. in the countries where banks have failed, small businesses have been particularly affected. this, in turn, has crippled parts of the real economy. the system shouldn't be that sensitive. it would also be useful if a number of crossborder barriers could be broken down. this part of the single market is seriously underutilized. there is a great european potential that is just waiting to be tapped. at the same time, it is important to be aware of the risks. the cmu must not develop into a purely deregulatory agenda for example in the area of securitisation. the weakening of key regulatory frameworks could jeopardise financial stability. we can't let that happen.

madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to say a few words about the acta agreement, because it seems that history is about to repeat itself. the acta was a trade agreement negotiated during the last mandate and, like ttip, it started with a unanimous decision by the member states. the opinion in parliament was very positive; it seemed as if it was a matter of course that the agreement would be passed. but the longer the negotiations progressed, the more the criticism from scientists and from popular movements grew. the commission published a document saying that everything was just myth. the document was called 'ten myths about acta', which of course turned out to be incorrect. by the time the vote was taken, public opinion had been awakened. civil society was mobilised and parliamentary groups took a stand against the acta agreement. the vote count, three years ago on saturday, 478 against and 39 for. if the commission does not listen, the ttip will follow suit.
in writing. ‒ i am sceptical about the value of the new efsi fund, especially considering that some of the money is taken from forward-looking programmes with clear european added value such as horizon 2020 and the connecting europe facility. but now that the fund is in place, we have to make the best of it, for example, how it can be used for infrastructure in sweden.
‒ today, parliament voted on an own-initiative report on the general situation of dairy farmers in the eu and the impact of the abolition of milk quotas. the situation is serious, not least in sweden, where on average one dairy farmer a day is laid off. i voted in favour of the report because it is characterized by a sense of crisis which the situation undeniably demands. but also because the report is clear that it was right to abolish the milk quota system. another important point in the report is the importance of reducing bureaucracy and administrative burdens for milk producers. the christian democrats want to see a fundamentally changed european agricultural policy, in which deregulation and natural competitiveness play a more prominent role. that is why i voted against many parts of the report. for example, demands for an increase in the intervention price for milk (price regulation), criticism of globalisation and demands for new subsidy mechanisms.
‒ (m) supports the abolition of the european milk quota system. in the long run, it is good to let supply and demand drive this market. however, now in the transitional phase, it creates some negative consequences with lack of profitability and closures. (m) to protect a vibrant countryside and to make it possible to live throughout sweden. we therefore voted in favour of this report, which aims to mitigate these negative effects. however, we do not believe that trade restrictions and various forms of financial support are sustainable solutions in the long term.

on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, allow me first of all to thank mr cofferati and all the other colleagues for the hard work and good cooperation in improving this important legislative proposal. i am very happy that we have been able to find a compromise, in the centre of this european parliament, that can gather broad support among pro-european and progressive forces. this has, of course, meant that all of us have had to make concessions and do so in a spirit of compromise on a number of points, but i believe that the end result is acceptable to all of us. for the liberal group it has been a paramount concern that this directive should improve the ability for shareholders to exercise their rights in cross-border situations and not to add unnecessary red tape and bureaucracy, because let’s face it, european entrepreneurs and companies will not flourish because of the additional rules to comply with, but rather if we create a good business environment where they can focus on their core tasks. we do not believe that we can generate long-term engagement in companies by investors through legislative proposals requiring various sorts of policy papers or creative tax incentives. shareholders need access to appropriate information, but then it remains their responsibility to engage with the companies that they own. if this interest is not there, it cannot artificially be engineered by politicians or bureaucrats. i think that the focus put to a large extent by the proposals of the commission on requiring a detailed remuneration policy and remuneration report for the director’s remuneration is, to say the least, difficult to understand, not to say rather populist. although i would personally have been happy to remove even more, i am happy that the compromise proposals have respected the key points that were problematic. today i feel a lot more comfortable, and i ask my group to support the compromises and the report, and i look forward to the trilogues and the continued work on this file, fighting for a directive that is fit for purpose and focuses on shareholders’ rights rather than shareholders’ obligations.

madam president, i would first like to congratulate the rapporteur. i support his compromise. i am personally convinced that the prohibition of trade in seal products is moral and right, that animals have suffered immensely in the traditional slaughter. on the other hand, it has already been clearly stated here that there is one exception to this prohibition. i want to state in general that if a legal prohibition has an exception, it often causes problems in practice, often such a prohibition is circumvented or interpreted differently. i am a little concerned when i look at the exemption granted to the inuit, that it contains so many conditions and that it is somehow so complex that it will be up to the european commission and i therefore urge the commissioner to monitor very closely whether this exemption is being respected. it should be said that the inuit are not given a place for commercial trade in these goods. it clearly states that this is to be a hunt that is more for non-commercial purposes. that the hunt should be conducted humanely, and so on. it will therefore be for the commission to monitor this so that the exception to the legal prohibition is respected and that the interests of the animals on the one hand and the interests of the traditional inuit community on the other are really balanced. i therefore ask the commission not to end the matter with the adoption of this legislation but to monitor carefully whether what has been established as an exception is being respected. answer the question by raising the blue card. we are not in dispute, i am merely pointing out as a lawyer that in my view the exception is legally complex and that it is now up to the european commission, once the exception is in force, to check whether it is being respected. i'm saying that as a politician, i respect that the inuit community should have the right to hunt seals and to have that right within the framework of their traditional functioning and their traditional livelihood. but i also note that the commission will have to continue to monitor whether all aspects of this authorisation, this exemption, are being respected. whether hunting is humane, etc. we're not at odds.

madam president, i would like to thank you. so now we are going to ban trade in seal products, including ethically and sustainably hunted seals from, for example, sweden. let me be clear, because this is stumble. we will continue to hunt seals, more and more seals, which is something the commission itself recognizes in the same breath as proposing to ban trade in them. the stock is increasing. so, normal, responsible wildlife management requires us to hunt seals, but now we're going to kill the seals and then we're going to throw them away, burn them, bury them. what's going to become law is completely unethical. the new ban is justified by the protests of the world trade organisation, but there were several ways of addressing these problems, something we in the committee on trade pointed out. let me just point out three solutions that are better than this one, which is the worst of all solutions. the ban could have been lifted altogether. the trade in unethically hunted seals could have been banned. or it could have been made clear that the ban would not apply to seals that have been hunted for non-commercial purposes. this proposal is completely unreasonable. we have just introduced a ban on catching fish and throwing the dead back into the sea, but with this requirement it will be a practical law to do the same with seals. that's why we should do it all over again. tear it down, make it new, because we can't have it this way.
madam president, i would like to thank you. sustainable management of marine resources is of utmost importance, not least in the baltic sea, which is a sensitive inland sea. the mrm exemption that has existed is being removed with this decision, which is extremely unfortunate. the number of grey seals in the baltic sea is increasing all the time. we must continue to shoot seals to maintain the ecological balance of the baltic sea. the problem i have raised in committee all along when i am opposed to the removal of this exception is that it is difficult to get hunters when we are going to hunt seals. the county councils in sweden, which are responsible for making decisions and making sure that we hunt the seal that must be hunted, are already having a hard time finding serious hunters. because serious hunters want to take advantage of what they catch, not least in this case sealskins, which are actually tradable. what's gonna be the problem is we can't get hunters for the hunt that we have to do. the problem is, we can't call in the army to shoot seals. what will happen? my worst-case scenario is that we get a number of event companies around the baltic sea offering weekends where you can shoot wildlife at live animals without having to take responsibility for finding them. this is a bad decision.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the commissioner for doing a very good job, which is well balanced and in line with the research. i also want to make it clear that we in the alde group are in favour of a temporary ban on cloning. the difference in the debate today is very much whether it should be a permanent and total ban, or whether we should have a temporary ban. the nature of the temporary ban is that as technology may develop with the future and we make scientific progress, then of course we have to make some considerations and see if we need to change our legislation. in this respect, i think the commissioner has made a much better balance than our two main rapporteurs in the european parliament. the most problematic for me is when we make the great expansion of the concept to include the offspring of cloned animals. here we see that there is a fairly large part of european agricultural production already today, where we import genetic material for our own meat production or milk production. so you're probably eating cheese or drinking milk that comes from the offspring of a cloned animal every day. there is a scientific fear as soon as we look at our agricultural sector. but if we are to be able to feed more mouths on this earth, we must continually improve our methods and make european agriculture more efficient in the future. however, this must not be at the expense of animal welfare. we are therefore in favour of a temporary ban, but not an indefinite and long-term ban, which in my view is not based on any kind of scientific basis but is simply driven by people's fear of science in agricultural production. from the day we started to domesticate animals, to have domestic animals, we've been trying to breed and increase production, get more meat from our beef animals, get more milk from our dairy animals. even our crops have been improved and made more viable. we've been refining them continuously. we must be able to continue to do so in the future. that is why we support the commissioner's line. (blue card) thank you very much for your question, which is justified in several ways. i would like to ask you a counter question, though. we haven't developed an effective antibiotic in 20 years, almost 30 years. does this mean we should stop researching antibiotics? no, not at all! but we must constantly evaluate what the research is coming up with and be prepared to adapt to it. before antibiotics were invented, would they not have been investigated because no progress had been made in the centuries before? no, absolutely not, but we have to constantly research and constantly evaluate.
madam president, i would like to thank you. of course, there are challenges with cloning. the early technology created suffering for the animals. we need a broad gene pool to meet the challenges of the future. it is a sensitive issue, because public opinion is sceptical, as is so often the case with new technology. i therefore understand the commission's cautious attitude on this matter. but to demand, as the european parliament is now doing, a total ban on food from clones and their descendants for all future generations is downright irresponsible. i'll give you three reasons why: firstly, it is unscientific and anti-technological because technology is evolving and advancing. to say that there has been no development in this area in 30 years is downright dishonest. if that were the case, we would not be having this debate today. secondly, it's impossible to control, because we don't eat anything that's clones, except possibly their offspring. how would we know if an animal's ancestor's ancestor was a clone? it's as impossible as knowing if someone's great-great-grandfather was a fraternal twin. my third reason is that this in practice only means a ban on the import of food from more and more countries in the world. protectionists may think that's a good thing, but don't forget we are net exporters of food. our prohibition against them will be met by their prohibition against us. i am concerned that this risks being the first step towards a failure as monumental as the eu's gmo policy - a hard, populist, unscientific blow to the wind, which will be led by many other debates and many new problems.
in writing. ‒ i voted against the proposal because it is bad for sweden and the baltic sea region. this means that shot seals will no longer be marketed in sweden. it will reduce the incentives for the necessary wildlife management of the baltic sea. as regards fish, there is an eu-wide ban on discarding fish, known as the discard ban. the seals will therefore now be disposed of by compulsory disposal. it's beyond my comprehension.
‒ i fully support the wto decision in the seal products case, especially as animal welfare considerations have been the basis for the decision. it is very unfortunate, however, that the implementation proposed by the commission does not take account of the special circumstances prevailing in sweden. as a result of this decision, meat and skins from the permitted and necessary for the fisheries industry conservation hunt will not be used. it is a waste of natural resources not to be able to use the catch from the conservation hunt, and it will have a negative impact on the fishing industry and the archipelago environment. i have therefore voted against the motion.
‒ today the european parliament voted on a negotiating mandate on animal cloning. cloning is a complex issue and i believe that, in principle, cloning should not be allowed on the european market. however, the legislative procedure needs to be conducted with caution and a thorough analysis of possible consequences. the commission has proposed temporary legislation with an evaluation mechanism, which is basically a good idea. however, in the report before parliament this had been deleted and replaced by a requirement for the introduction of permanent legislation. the report also proposed a ban on the trade in meat from cloned animals. since the offspring of a clone does not differ in dna from a 'normal' animal, it would be a prohibition which would be difficult to enforce and which could result in undesirable effects for third countries. i voted against these problematic points and against the report as a whole because the line i advocate was voted down. i would like to stress that this should not be interpreted as support for animal cloning.
‒ this week the european parliament voted on a negotiating mandate on the cloning of animals for animal production. cloning is a complicated issue. it is important not to override animal welfare. the proposal on which the european parliament voted, however, contains major problems concerning the wto agreements and other trade-related aspects. moreover, it is not possible to solve the traceability problem technically, since cloned animals have identical dna to the original animal. the commission has therefore voted against the proposal, as it is not feasible and risks leading to major trade policy implications.
‒ i fully support the commission's proposal to ban the cloning of animals and the sale of products from cloned animals on the european market. there are clear animal health problems with the procedure and until those problems are resolved, a ban should apply. the rapporteur's proposal to extend the ban to include the offspring of cloned animals is very problematic. there are already a lot of offspring of cloned animals in europe, and these animals have no health problems. the proposal also raises major problems for imports from third countries. the rapporteur has adopted a position which is very far from the council's position and, moreover, far from what is practicable. this will make further discussions very problematic and risk stalling the whole legislative process. the likely result of the parliament's decision is that the area remains completely unregulated. as the liberal group's negotiator, i took a line which meant returning to the commission's proposal, and i have also voted against it in the final vote.
‒ this week the european parliament voted on a negotiating mandate on the cloning of animals for animal production. cloning is a complicated issue. it is important not to override animal welfare. the proposal on which the european parliament voted, however, contains major problems concerning the wto agreements and other trade-related aspects. moreover, it is not possible to solve the traceability problem technically, since cloned animals have identical dna to the original animal. the commission has therefore voted against the proposal, as it is not feasible and risks leading to major trade policy implications.

‒ today the european parliament voted on a report on fundamental rights in the eu, drawn up by laura ferrara of the italian five star movement. i chose to vote for an alternative proposal put forward by my group, the epp. unfortunately, the epp motion was not put to the vote, and i chose to vote against the ferrara report. the reason is that the report is contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. everything from a federal definition of marriage to legislation on corporate board quota and the abortion issue is covered in the text. these are issues that should be decided at national level and not in the eu institutions. on the abortion issue, the christian democrats believe that there are two individuals worth protecting in a pregnancy and that it is therefore in the interest of society to keep the number of abortions down. that perspective is completely missing from laura ferrara's report.
in writing. - i am in favour of full respect for fundamental rights. i supported many aspects of the report, particularly those relating to children's rights, sexual and reproductive rights, combating violence against women and lgbti rights. i abstained in the final vote because many other aspects should be dealt with at national level, such as the directive on maternity leave. i also do not support the new mechanism for monitoring respect for fundamental rights. it is the commission's task as guardian of the treaties to assess whether member states are complying with their obligations. the european parliament should not become a court of law. fundamental rights should never be politicized.
in writing. the fact that information and communication technologies (ict) should not be misused and contribute to the violation of human rights is a matter of course for us swedish social democrats. we believe that the eu and the ict companies operating in the eu should take responsibility for ensuring this in their relations with third countries, which is why we chose to support the report. we believe that the text clearly emphasizes the importance of all forms of digital surveillance being carried out with respect for fundamental rights and under effective parliamentary control. however, we would like to emphasise the importance of this further, as the technological and digital tools available today can also act as important tools in the fight against terrorism and organised crime, provided that this is done in a controlled manner and with respect for human privacy and digital freedom.

for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. we will be taking a position tomorrow on fisheries agreements between the eu and cape verde, madagascar and guinea-bissau. madagascar and cape verde are tuna fisheries and are relatively well designed, so we will give our support to these agreements. the main problem with the tuna agreements is that over the years they have come to include increasingly large by-catches of sharks. in these two agreements in particular, this has been addressed and there are clauses in both agreements to reduce by-catch of sharks. we will therefore support these agreements. as regards the protocol with guinea-bissau, we think it is a hasty one and are very disappointed with the way it has been drawn up. this so-called new protocol negotiated after the political situation in guinea-bissau has stabilised is nothing but an old protocol. it looks exactly like the protocol that was suspended in 2012, when there was a military coup in guinea bissau. a lot has happened in the last few years. new chinese boats have been added, fraudulent tonnage certificates have been used, and illegal fishing has been reported. the commission was in such a hurry when it was free to renegotiate that it did not consider that it had time to make a new evaluation. it's all based on the old evaluation from 2011. how can we make a fair assessment if we are really only fishing for the surplus that guinea-bissau cannot itself exploit? this is one of the basic rules of the new external fisheries policy in the new reform. moreover, the political situation is worrying to say the least. the government was recently dissolved in august 2015, and there are allegations of corruption that also apply to fishing licenses. we shall not vote in favour of the conclusion of this protocol with guinea-bissau.

the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. president. - the debate is closed. each environmental law is the result of a great effort by civil society, organisations, politicians and citizens. but what exactly happens in the next line? the statistics speak their own language. we see that most of the new cases of delayed transposition initiated by the commission were environmental related a total of 168 cases. i think it is regrettable, it is dangerous for the environment and it is an insult to all the people who have put a lot of energy into improving environmental policy. these are the problems we are addressing in our report. firstly, we criticise the fact that environmental legislation is all too often implemented too late. secondly, we demand that the commission be more strict with those member states which do not comply. thirdly, we must make it clear that the large number of members should not lower the level of ambition. i would also like to mention that we are clear about the refit and that we do not want it to lead to lower ambitions.
firstvice-president of the commission.– madam president, first of all let me pick up on the last point that was made and let me quote the great greek philosopher socrates about this: in the rule of law, if it is to work, all laws need to be applied. it is not an à la carte menu. if you want the rule of law to function and be respected by everyone, it should apply equally to everyone in all cases. that is what the commission’s role is: to make sure that eu law is applied according to what was agreed by the legislators, including when it is transposed into national legislation. one member raised the issue of the application of environmental law: the issue here is, indeed, partly one of late application but, unfortunately, in a larger number of cases it is about the wrong application of eu directives. the commission then has to start infringement procedures – and we do start infringement procedures because i strongly believe that, if we do not, you undermine the very principle socrates referred to, namely the application of all laws to everyone, regardless of their position, or regardless of the member state. i insist on this because i believe this commission will need to intensify its operations in making sure that eu law is applied correctly in all member states. secondly, i would like to pick up on a point about refit. better regulation makes an immediate contribution to the better enforcement of eu law. by carefully assessing the impact of new legislation – and mr kelly referred to this as well – and by consulting widely, we ensure that the legislation we produce will be clear, workable and proportionate to the aims we pursue. by producing legislation of the highest quality and by ensuring that our acquis remains fit for purpose, we will also ensure that problems of enforcement are reduced. to make that clearer: in the beginning, if you are sure that you are not just looking for a compromise in this house but you are also looking for something that can be applied in a transparent and efficient way, you will create fewer problems afterwards. all three institutions involved – parliament, council and commission – have a shared responsibility. i hope that we will soon conclude a new interinstitutional agreement on better law making, which will help us together to produce legislation that will serve the european citizen in the best possible way. a lot of complaints about red tape are not about the amount of legislation but rather about the quality of legislation which leads to undue administrative burdens. in his oral presentation, the rapporteur went deeply into the issue of austerity programmes. i did not see much of that in the report, but i did see it very much in his presentation. so let me go into that briefly: i strongly believe that economic adjustment programmes do not reduce the capacity of member state authorities to comply with eu law. on the contrary, if you do not observe fiscal discipline over a longer period of time, you will fatally undermine the capacity of the state to provide social programmes to ensure the possibility of a welfare state. it is the triangle of fiscal responsibility, structural reform and investment. you cannot pick and choose. you cannot just say that you will only invest and will disregard fiscal responsibility – which is sometimes what people on parts of the left are saying – and you cannot just say that it is all about fiscal responsibility and we do not need investment – which parts of the right are saying. you need a balance in this triangle, as was stressed again by president juncker last night. the greek and cypriot memoranda of understanding were not part of eu law, because they led to intergovernmental financial support – in contrast, by the way, to the irish and portuguese ones, which received support from the union budget. the greek and cypriot mous were formally agreed only between the lenders and the beneficiary member state. the court of justice confirmed that, when adopting and implementing measures under such an intergovernmental adjustment programmes, the national authorities of the member state concerned are not implementing eu law. i think this point needs to be assessed carefully when members discuss the report.
member of the commission.– madam president, let me start by expressing my thanks to mrs bearder for having taken this initiative and submitted this question. first of all, i would like to thank all members of this house for your constant and keen interest in addressing trafficking in human beings and keeping it high on the european union’s political agenda. let us be clear, trafficking in human beings is about the buying and selling of people and their services. trafficking in human beings is expressly prohibited in the european union’s charter of fundamental rights and addressing trafficking means upholding fundamental rights. at the same time, this is a serious form of organised crime as clearly stipulated in the european union’s treaties. this deplorable crime would not exist without the huge profits for traffickers and the demand that drives all forms of exploitation. if we want to prevent more victims falling prey to traffickers we must follow the money, both in the legal and the illegal sectors, and we must stop the flows. trafficking in human beings is now an integral part of the european agenda on migration and also of the european agenda on security. indeed, it is a phenomenon with clear links to many policy areas within and outside the european union. eurostat data indicate that for 2010-2012 the 28 member states registered 30 146 victims. eighty per cent of the victims were female and 70% of traffickers were male. sixteen per cent of registered victims were children: girls and boys. data prove that the most widespread form of exploitation is sexual exploitation – up to 69%, of which women and girls form the vast majority of the victims: 95%. over 1 000 child victims were trafficked for sexual exploitation. these data clearly demonstrate a strong gender dimension to trafficking in human beings. let me be clear, the european union has recognised that trafficking in human beings is a structural form of violence against women. it seriously inhibits women’s ability to enjoy rights and freedom on an equal basis with men. gender plays a role at every stage of the trafficking process by increasing vulnerability to trafficking, by fuelling exploitation and influencing the effectiveness of assistance and support. i am proud to say that the commission has delivered a great deal and my services continue to deliver very ambitiously on work in this direction. we have a comprehensive legal and policy framework guided by the european union’s anti-trafficking directive and the eu strategy towards the eradication of trafficking in human beings 2012-2016. the anti-trafficking directive is human-rights based and victim-centred. it is gender specific and child-sensitive. the deadline for transposition of this directive expired on 6 april 2013. the commission, as guardian of the european union treaties, initiated procedures without delay and i am pleased to inform you that, to date, 25 member states have officially informed the commission that they have fully transposed the directive at national level. only two member states have not notified the commission yet. my services are currently analysing the information provided by all member states and we will not hesitate to take all necessary steps to ensure full transposition and application of the european union’s law. the commission will be submitting the following reports to parliament and to the council. first, in early 2016, the first european union report on the progress made in addressing trafficking in human beings, as per article 20 of the directive, will be presented. member states and civil society, as represented in the european union’s civil society platform, have submitted information to the eu anti-trafficking coordinator as per article 20 and the report is being prepared with my services. this report will address the gender dimension and relevant considerations. secondly, the commission will submit a unified report under article 23 of the directive in 2016. this report will assess the measures taken by member states to comply with the anti-trafficking directive. it will also assess the impact existing national laws criminalising those who use the services of victims of trafficking have on prevention of this crime, accompanied if necessary by appropriate proposals. this report will equally address the gender dimension of trafficking in human beings, including, particularly, the measures to reduce the demand that drives all forms of exploitation. the european union’s anti-trafficking directive sets forth the legal obligation to reduce the demand that fuels exploitation, including by considering criminalising those who knowingly use the services of victims. the employers sanctions directive already criminalises those who knowingly employ victims of trafficking who are third-country nationals. my services have commissioned a study on the gender dimensions of trafficking in human beings from a renowned academic institution to examine the gender dimensions of trafficking for the purpose of sexual exploitation and measures to reduce the demand that fuels this exploitation. the study also took into consideration the recommendation of this parliament in its resolution of 26 february 2014 on sexual exploitation and prostitution and its impact on gender equality. the study will be finalised by the end of this year. the european union anti-trafficking strategy comes to an end in 2016. as stipulated in the european agendas for security and for migration, we will adopt a post-2016 strategy. we will ensure that we consult old stakeholders and ensure that parliament is kept abreast of developments. the gender dimension must be present in our post-2016 approach. ladies and gentlemen, it is imperative to continue delivering. we owe this to the victims and, of course, to our societies, to our children and to the future generations.
on behalf of the ppe group.– madam president, trafficking of human beings has become the new slavery of our time. the figures mentioned by the commissioner are stunning and the human stories are heart-breaking. commissioner, the epp group really welcomes your determination to ensure full compliance with a clear gender perspective. we are all concerned. this is serious organised crime with a cross-border dimension and it should be treated as such by all member states. the network requires a common european response, with increased police cooperation and increased judiciary cooperation with europol, eurojust and frontex. it is really time to break the circle of impunity. there should be no hiding place for these criminals in and around europe. we are calling on you, commissioner, to present as soon as possible the report on compliance, ensuring a clear gender perspective because, as you said, sexual exploitation is one of the biggest causes of trafficking, as are the protection of victims (girls, women and people with special needs), reducing demand and supply and holding the users responsible. the majority of victims of trafficking in human beings continue to be women and girls, and now increasingly migrants. therefore, your new strategy against trafficking should, as you said, have a clear gender perspective: criminalise, strengthening cooperation and tracking funds. the epp group continues to be committed to zero tolerance against trafficking of human beings and keeping gender rights on the agenda. no one should be mistreated, abused or exploited on their way to safety in europe.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group.– madam president, i am happy to hear that the commissioner will be focusing on addressing demand, but what is demand in this area? it is brothel owners and pimps, of course, but it is also men buying sex, and as in all other areas we have to address and target the market. as the honeyball report voted here last year concluded, the nordic model criminalising both the pimps and the buying of sex services has been very effective in addressing the demand. so, what should the commission be doing in targeting the demand for buying women and girls in the sex industry? so far, not enough has been done. i have read about the piece of research that has been awarded to a group of lobbyists and under-qualified researchers. i am very worried about this, and i think it is not serious. to put it bluntly, we do not need more police officers and border controls in this struggle. we need fewer brothels. so we look forward to a constructive dialogue with you to see what actions we can improve in order to make a difference for women and girls in europe.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the trafficking in human beings directive adopted in 2011 directly addresses the fact that trafficking in human beings is gender specific and affects men and women differently. what this really means is that women are affected to a much, much greater extent than men by trafficking. in particular, in the case of trafficking in human beings for sexual purposes, which is also the most common form of trafficking in human beings, women and girls represent a clear majority. this fact means that women and girls are threatened with being sold in a market where their bodies are considered a commodity that men have the right to buy and exploit. we can no longer turn a blind eye to this kind of oppression and exploitation of people. indeed, it is now time for the commission to live up to its promise to evaluate the implementation by member states of the directive adopted in 2011. it is time for the commission to take a strong stance on this issue, and then a review of the member states' actions is the least i can ask for. sex is not a human right. on the other hand, human trafficking is the most heinous crime of our time, destroying the lives of women and girls. i therefore turn to the commission and the member states of the european union and say: that's enough!

in writing. ‒ i supported the resolution on the refugee crisis in the eu. it is a common challenge that needs a european response. we have to move from emergency measures to a permanent crisis mechanism of distribution of refugees. all ms should take their responsibility and fully apply the common asylum package. the best interest of the child should be always respected in all procedures. we need to increase our cooperation in order to be able to save lives and assist member states under pressure with easo, frontex and hotspots. we also need to combat traffickers and smugglers who exploit people's desperation. europol and eurojust can play a big role in coordinating action. reception centres in third countries, however, where asylum procedures would be initiated, are not realistic at the moment. instead we should increase funding for unchr, wfp and humanitarian aid to help refugees in neighbouring countries.
‒ i voted in favour of the resolution, which was negotiated by my epp group together with the other major groups. the resolution is broadly balanced, but it lacks clear condemnation of the hamas terror regime in gaza, the lack of democracy and freedom in the plo-controlled west bank, the responsibility of the surrounding arab dictatorships for supporting terrorism and the perpetuation of the refugee problem. the reports on iran and the agreement on its nuclear programme also express an optimism which i fear is unfounded. the iranian regime has not changed its character: its leadership still wants the destruction of israel and considers the west to be a mortal enemy. the resolution should therefore have contained a firm demand that any lifting of the other sanctions not related to the country's nuclear activities should not be lifted unless iran respects human rights. this was in the light of the fact that the iranian leadership had expressed the view that the nuclear agreement should be interpreted as a basis for the lifting of all sanctions against the country.
in writing. - i voted against the report. the whole report focuses on flexibility and mobility. the report says that unemployment is more about the unemployed and mobility. there is no awareness of the eu's own austerity policies as a key cause of mass unemployment. the report also shows a lack of respect for the role of the trade union movement in the labour market. that's why i voted no.
‒ a competitive eu labour market well adapted to a modern and changing labour market is crucial for the future competitiveness of the eu. this own-initiative report, with a rapporteur from the alde group, was therefore very welcome. calls for linking education more closely to labour markets, ensuring labour market mobility and reducing the tax burden on labour are important and necessary measures which i support. as a centre party member and defender of the swedish model, however, i could not support paragraph 106 on a european framework for minimum wages. this is something that the social partners in sweden should deal with, and i therefore voted against that proposal. the vote on paragraph 106 was split, and the final vote considered that the question of minimum wages should be dealt with in accordance with national practice, which i think is to leave the matter to the social partners. that is why i voted in favour of the report, since its proposals would radically improve the eu's labour market, which in turn would lead to increased growth and help the eu to emerge from the economic crisis.
