author.– madam president, the origin of this resolution and this debate that we are having tonight is numerous petitions that the committee on petitions has received during the past few years. as chair of this committee i fully understand, and regret to hear of, the difficulties faced by petitioners. most petitions concern the situation in spain but the same problems are also known in other member states. the committee on petitions is the bridge between eu citizens and eu institutions and, as its chair, i strive for the eu to better take advantage of citizens’ direct experiences of eu law and also the experiences of national implementation, because these experiences can help us as legislators to further improve future legislation. in this role of bringing the concerns of citizens forward to this house, we have initiated this debate tonight. as i said, we have received a large number of petitions denouncing the violation of fundamental rights and infringement of eu legislation in connection with the lack of accurate consumer information related to practices in mortgage banking. obtaining accurate information is crucial for making sound economic decisions, and of course vital when we talk about important personal investments for an individual citizen and his or her family. these petitions highlight a number of abusive clauses and practices in the spanish mortgage sector which have been identified by both national and european courts. a large number of citizens became victims of financial fraud as their savings banks sold them very risky financial instruments and they were not duly informed about the extent of the risks involved in the proposed investment; nor did the banks perform suitably tests to determine whether clients had the knowledge to understand the risk that they were undertaking. this has led to cases of terrible personal suffering and huge economic losses, where citizens have lost their home and all their life savings. many tragedies have taken place before our eyes. however, i would like to stress that the aim of this question and the discussion tonight should be seen in a wider perspective. our aim is to raise awareness and seek clarity regarding a general problem of bad banking practices that can be found all over europe, not only within spain. i would therefore like to stress that transparency and informed consumer protection will be essential components if we want to combat abusive terms in mortgage contracts and thus strengthen protection for borrowers. nevertheless, on behalf of my committee, i would like to ask the commission some questions related to this situation. i hope that the commission’s investigation and conclusions will allow us to improve the situation for the petitioners and also to ensure that we can avoid this kind of practice in the future and consequently improve the application of european union law. so, commissioner, on the basis of the petitions, a number of questions should have an answer tonight. do the petitions relate to the breach of banks’ obligations in relation to the information to be provided to clients under the markets in financial instruments directive (mifid) i, as transposed by spanish law? has spain properly transposed mifid i or have spanish national authorities properly enforced the applicable provisions? we are also asking what measures the commission has taken or will take to address the abuses committed by companies selling financial instruments that affect small depositors and small owners of financial instruments such as preferred shares, which in many cases have been marketed without full compliance with eu legislation. i would like to know, too, if the commission is willing to take measures with regard to unfair clauses in spanish mortgage law, in particular with regard to unfair commercial practices and the incorrect behaviour of the banks that provided all these people with the loans in question. i am looking forward to your answers, commissioner jourová, and also to hearing the views of my colleagues. and let me say, on a personal note, that we should be full of compassion for the people who have suffered terribly, but we should also make sure that future legislation is safe and sound for everyone in europe.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. eu peace and security policy should be about development, trade, human rights and much more and it is interrelated. that is why we often have a clash when we talk about the export of war material. i am not only referring to conventional arms exports, but also to the risks of dual-use products, which are not always regulated today. i also speak of the risks involved in exporting all this material to non-democratic states. many times, these are materials that can lead to greater security, but if they fall into the wrong hands, they can threaten both our security and human rights to the same extent as conventional weapons. the export of information technology and surveillance equipment to regimes that violate human rights is particularly risky. the arab spring has shown this, for example, with nokia and siemens helping the iranian regime to persecute democracy activists. swedish ericsson chose to provide the syrian regime with telecommunications equipment when the conflict began, which meant that oppositionists could be easily tracked by the regime. german and british technology was used against protesters by the regimes in bahrain, egypt and tunisia. democratic organisations with official eu support have been spied on, infiltrated, members tortured, killed or imprisoned. it is therefore very important that we address the export of sensitive information and communication technologies to these countries. i hope that in its review the commission will also look to find a very strict and consistent regulatory framework to prevent rather than come and put out fires.

mr president, people fleeing to europe should be treated with dignity, by everyone. we have to step up our common humanitarian efforts, supporting jordan, lebanon and turkey better, as well as serbia and macedonia. we need to give more funding to international organisations like unhcr and the world food programme, and increase regional support programmes. it is not enough to pledge money: funding has to be disbursed fast and through a simple procedure, and humanitarian support on the spot is not enough. i led the delegation of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe) to lampedusa and our findings were that the search and rescue operation in the central mediterranean is proving effective thanks to coordination between the italian operation mare sicuro, eunavfor med, triton and the ngos. we also found that military means and eu cooperation in humanitarian operations are essential to save lives and to combat smugglers. however, smugglers are changing their modus operandi and we have to change our mandate: to broaden it and also to focus more on the eastern route from turkey and egypt to greece and southern italy. there are legal ways in: the problem is that the member states do not implement them. resettlement is the highway in, and it is time to move from a voluntary resettlement to a binding resettlement system. it is also important that all member states apply the asylum package, including registration and fingerprinting at the external borders of the european union. this is essential in order for freedom of movement and schengen to function.

in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of this report today. i am doing this in order to provide workers in these cases with financial support for retraining and/or other measures to find new jobs. however, i object to the procedure of the committee on budgets to appoint a rapporteur from the region/electoral district to which the aid is directed. that's a clear conflict of interest. i would also like to stress that the widespread unemployment in the eu could be tackled more effectively if the austerity policies long pursued by the european commission and the governments of the member states were abandoned and a policy of public, green investment was pursued instead.
in writing. ‒ we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we regard as an expression of protectionism. we have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the exclusion that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in the light of the swedish national audit office's analysis, which suggests that the fund does indeed have a negative impact on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its legitimacy and should not be used any more.
in writing. ‒ we moderates are critical of the globalisation fund as such and have voted against its creation. we have also opposed the eu's allocation of funds for this purpose in budgetary votes. however, we do not dispute individual payments once the fund is in place.
in writing. ‒ i am convinced that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. that is why i am opposed in principle to the european globalisation adjustment fund, which i regard as an expression of protectionism. we in the people's party have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the alienation that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in the light of the swedish national audit office's analysis, which suggests that the fund does indeed have a negative impact on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its legitimacy and should not be used any more.
‒ today we voted on financial aid from the so-called globalisation fund to people who have become unemployed after european companies have closed down. i am consistently voting no to these unemployment benefits for two reasons: first, labour market policies and financial support for the unemployed should be decided on, and financed, at national level not at eu level. secondly, the fund's basic idea is that globalisation is something negative - which is not true. globalization and free trade lead to growth and peace.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of this report today. i am doing this in order to provide workers in these cases with financial support for retraining and/or other measures to find new jobs. however, i would like to stress that the widespread unemployment in the eu could be effectively combated if the austerity policies pursued for a long time by the european commission and the governments of the member states were stopped. to create new jobs in europe, a new economic policy with massive public and environmentally friendly investments is needed. investments for the benefit of society are needed in education, health and care, but also in infrastructure, railways, public transport, energy-efficient housing and sustainable energy systems.

for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. according to amnesty international, most executions today take place in communist china. then there are the dictatorships of iran and saudi arabia. in saudi arabia, executions are considered the only form of public entertainment, besides football. as in the case of the islamic state, executions are carried out by beheading and crucifixion. to hear the stories and the images of these executions is a study in evil and barbarism. at the same time, the question of the death penalty cannot be reduced to a question of procedure. the fundamental issue for any rule of law must be about human dignity. according to the judeo-christian ethic upon which our civilization is founded, criminals should be punished, atone for their actions, and then given a second chance. the society condemns the crime, but not the criminal. the death penalty does not make that distinction. the death penalty violates the fundamental principle of the right to life. today, as the european parliament pays attention to the 13th world day against the death penalty, it is urgent to highlight the values on which the european union is founded and our duty to always put human dignity first, based on those values.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there is nothing as definite as death, nothing as irrevocable. everyone, including the courts, makes mistakes. in most lands it sometimes happens that a person in prison, after many, many years, asks for and receives a resurrection when new evidence may show that he has been innocently convicted. but death is irreversible. there is no way back to life once that punishment has been meted out. the state's job is to protect, not end people's lives. the death penalty is brutal. it is uncivilized, it is illegitimate and it is inhumane. i therefore appeal today to both the presidency and the commission, which shines with its absence, to make it an absolute priority to ensure that we contribute to its eradication wherever it still exists in the world.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, today we are discussing the capital markets union (cmu), which is part of the european investment plan. as we have said several times before, the investment plan has three parts – the european fund for strategic investment (efsi), the project portal and the single market – and the capital markets union is part of this third pillar, deepening and widening the internal market. i personally believe that the third pillar of the plan is the most powerful when we are talking about job creation and economic growth in europe. that is why the capital markets union represents a very important part of the plan when we are trying to get more investment to europe. a couple of figures to set out the scale of the challenge we have in creating a capital markets union: europe’s economy is about the same size as that of the usa but our equity market is less than half the size of theirs; in the usa, smes get about five times more funding from the capital markets or non-bank financing than they do here in the european union; if our venture capital markets were as well developed as they are in the united states, companies could have raised an extra eur 90 billion over the past five years; and, perhaps even more importantly, the differences between the eu member states are greater than the differences between the eu and the united states. so everybody understands that there is a lot of work to be done here in europe. allow me now to draw your attention to the following three important points. first, we have been listening very carefully to the feedback provided to us by stakeholders and the european parliament. the resolution you passed earlier this summer provided useful input, as did the consultation result from over 700 responses. personally, i have visited 27 member states in the context of the investment roadshow. i received important feedback as to what to fix in the single market and, more especially, in financial services. many features of this feedback are addressed in the action plan. second, this action plan is very ambitious. the plan will have a big impact, because we have adopted a holistic and inclusive approach, covering also the framework conditions as called for by the five presidents’ report on emu deepening. in addition to commissioner hill, who is the main contributor, other colleagues – commissioner jourová, commissioner moscovici and commissioner moedas – are playing important roles in the implementation of the plan. next year we will be making concrete legislative proposals on the tax side, via the common consolidated corporate tax base (ccctb), and on insolvency practices. moreover, we are fully exploiting the synergies that exist when using the different tools at our disposal. it is therefore important to judge the measures not just in isolation but also as part of the comprehensive package. i will give you a couple of examples. with the efsi we are financially supporting fresh investments in critical infrastructure projects. in parallel, the amendments to solvency ii that we are putting forward under cmu, to create a well-defined infrastructure asset class with appropriately calibrated capital requirements, should draw in long-term investors such as insurance companies. risk charges on infrastructure investment will come down by some 30%. we are also acting to re-launch a european securitisation market, both via the activities of the european fund for strategic investment and now through cmu. last week we made a proposal for a new framework to encourage the take-up of simple transparent and standardised securitisation (sts). this will define when a securitisation counts as sts, and again it will set lower capital requirements that will apply when it meets those criteria. if we could rebuild the securitisation market to the levels it was at before the crisis that could add up to an extra eur 100 billion of investment for the whole economy. with this in mind, i urge the council and parliament to take forward the proposed legislation without delay. honourable members, we managed to push the efsi regulation through in only six months. i hope parliament and the council can deliver as quickly on these proposals. these proposals are, in places, even more simple than the efsi regulation, so let us put private money to work for new jobs. looking especially at smes, here too we are working holistically to devise measures to help smes get financing on capital markets. we are looking at the prospectus, which is what businesses have to publish when they want to list on a stock exchange. unfortunately, they can be lengthy and expensive. so we will be bringing forward a proposal to modernise the prospectus directive so that these documents serve their initial purpose. they certainly need to provide clear information for investors, but they also need to be affordable for smes to produce. meanwhile, the european investment advisory hub, created under the umbrella of the efsi, will explore how to provide advisory help to smes when it comes to market experience. third, while ambitious, the action plan is credible and realistic. we are not making promises we cannot keep. we are not intervening or regulating where the case is not clear-cut and where national markets are working well. in areas where, for example, progress in the past has been difficult and where we feel more groundwork is still needed, or where the private sector is developing useful initiatives, we adopt a pragmatic step-by-step approach. this is, for example, the case for covered bond markets for private placements. personal pensions are another example. personal pensions have the potential to inject more savings into capital markets and to channel more of that money to productive investments. yet the eu does not have a single market for voluntary personal pensions, limiting choice and putting up prices. next year we will start work to determine exactly what is needed to establish a european market for simple personal pensions and we will clarify whether or not eu legislation could help to underpin that market. finally, let me stress that we will be watchful of emerging risks to financial stability. the commission is committed to working with the european supervisory authorities to strengthen supervisory convergence and to ensure that the rules of the game are interpreted with consistency across different jurisdictions. to conclude, the capital markets union action plan is a major step towards completing the single market in capital. the commission has delivered a comprehensive package on time. it is our strong desire now to work together with the council and with parliament to achieve swift agreements on our proposals for the benefit of the european economy and our citizens.
on behalf of the ppe group.– mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for this statement. as he touched upon eu–usa economic relations, i cannot but underline the fact that the european union is the bigger economy – even the us central intelligence agency recognises this. but, as the commissioner has pointed out, we do not have the magnitude and the strength of the capital markets that the united states has, and that is where we need to proceed. that is why it is very good that the commission has taken these initiatives. however, there are some things worth thinking about. the first is the gap between the liquidity we have in european markets and the lack of investments. in that perspective, something is wrong or missing. we need to look again at the regulations, and review what has been achieved over the last years in the area of regulation. it might be that some things can be done better, and it might be that some things are not as necessary as we thought they were. that is why a review is very important. i would also like to underline a difference between the usa and europe, and that is that we are, for historical reasons, more dependent on banks. that is why it is important to look upon european banks as part of the broader capital market. i would also like to say that it is important to look upon the fact that we have a number of well-functioning capital markets in the union. so an important task is to get rid of the hindrances that are making it more difficult to facilitate all the things we could do. finally, we have a number of well-functioning markets that could be used as a template and that we could use as an example. let us use them as an example and not over-regulate them. let us see to it that we regulate on the basis of using the strength of the capital markets that we have and will get in the future. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer.– of course we need to do that and i think an important part of that is to ensure that we can have profitable investments in the european market because that is the way to build up capital. but i would also like to underline that it is very important that banks can fulfil the whole line of financing and investment-making and market-making. because if we are changing the structures of european banks in order to reduce their opportunities to contribute to the financing of small and medium-sized enterprises, then we are making the problems worse. i think we should use what we have, but develop it and make it better.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. president. - the debate is closed. i am very pleased with the agreement on the payment services directive which we are voting on tomorrow. the negotiations have been tough. i would have preferred a stronger single market perspective and an even stronger consumer perspective. on the whole, however, the final compromise is a very good one. i would like to point out three improvements that the new rules bring. first, competition will be intensified. different types of non-bank actors will be able to compete with banks in a fairer and more reasonable manner. the so-called third parties, which offer payment initiation or account information, should be included in the regulatory framework on the same terms as any other. it creates security and safety for consumers. at the same time, it gives those operators the access they need to make the business model work. the banks should not be able to prevent them from providing their services. in addition, improved conditions for bank-independent payment systems will also be provided. new information requirements shall ensure that such systems operate more efficiently. all this will create a more healthy competitive environment in the payments market. this will in turn benefit both the consumer and society at large. secondly, the legislation will promote innovation in terms of both technological advances and business models. this will be an important effect of opening the doors to new players. we are now seeing the digital revolution sweep over the payments market as well. all indications are that a lot will happen in the next few years. the updated directive will serve as a solid foundation for this development. many consumers will benefit from improved payment services. i actually think it's perfect. thirdly, the new rules will also strengthen the role of consumers in other respects. these include significantly stricter rules on surcharges, stricter requirements for businesses in respect of transactions where the final amount is not known in advance and reducing the liability of honest consumers in the case of unauthorised transactions. the conclusion is clear. in a more competitive and innovative market, it will be easier, cheaper, safer for consumers to pay. it's clearly a step in the right direction. i would also like to take this opportunity to thank the rapporteur and all the shadow rapporteurs for their excellent cooperation in this process.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it has long been known that russia has used gas energy to influence various countries and stop their possible turn towards the west and the eu. some examples: in 2007, all supplies of petroleum products to estonia were cut in connection with the oil crisis. when the same country passed a law affecting estonian russians, it stopped the gas. when georgia elected a pro-western government, price increases. when ukraine did the same thing: price increases. when the monetary union with belarus failed: price increases. the baltic countries, armenia, kyrgyzstan, transnistria, georgia have all been hit by price increases. now it's the turn of the balkans. russia is in full swing in bosnia and serbia, locking those countries up too, with the goal of getting them to dance to the kremlin's tune. finally, we know that the planned expansion of nord stream will worsen the situation for ukraine. my question is: how will the commission ensure that this does not happen in the future? we saw it yesterday, we see it today. what means will the commission use to ensure that we do not see this again in the future?

in writing. we swedish social democrats chose to abstain in the vote on the resolution as a whole. we also chose to abstain on motions for resolutions on eu legislation on family insolvency and on the harmonisation of the terms of mortgage agreements and debt restructuring procedures. on the one hand, it is important to provide consumers with sound protection in the mortgage market. the development of the situation in spain, where many people have been forced to leave their homes in recent years, is very unfortunate. everyone must be given reasonable opportunities to deal with payment difficulties and debts. on the other hand, we are not convinced that eu legislation is the right way to go in these cases. on the contrary, we see problems. the use of the family as a legal entity can be misused in several respects, not least in terms of individual rights and gender equality. nor is it clear that eu insolvency rules would be in line with the principle of subsidiarity. in general, extended harmonisation in this area could lead to some eu countries being forced to reduce their consumer protection. that would not be acceptable.
‒ no one should have to face discrimination in the eu on the grounds of sex, colour or sexual orientation. this non-legislative report was an important milestone in safeguarding the rules and principles of anti-discrimination in the eu, and i therefore voted in favour of it. i would point out, however, that there were parts of the text which i did not vote for, such as the sections on quotas and questions of parental insurance at eu level.
regarding the wave of refugees, we need to be open to all those who flee from atrocities and criminality and who need to come to safety and security here. we need to combine that with firmness regarding our borders because that is a precondition for the handling of asylum. we need to reform in order to ensure that we can receive people and find them jobs so that they can be a part of society and also live where they arrive because otherwise we will have huge problems. but we also need to stabilise the situation in syria and iraq and in ukraine. we need to deal with both these challenges. i would like to underline that there is one common denominator and that is the power play and aggression of russia: in syria, siding with the terror of assad; in ukraine, warmongering over another country’s borders and still proceeding to destabilise another country. we need to meet both of these challenges. madam president, i would like to thank you. it is very positive that a global standard for the automatic exchange of account information has been established. it is also very good that this standard will be used in the eu at the beginning of next year. the new standard is an important tool against tax fraud and aggressive tax planning, and it is a major step forward. in this context, it is entirely logical to rapidly phase out the standard used in the context of the savings directive. maintaining dual reporting systems - i.e. both for the savings directive and the new standard - would not be appropriate in this case. it would provide a little more information, but the costs in terms of red tape and duplication of work would be far greater than the value of the information we get. over the next 3-4 years, it is vital that the european commission works actively and transparently to make the transition to the new global standard work. this is not only true between eu countries, but also in relation to the five european non-eu countries and the 12 territories which are also to move from the savings directive standard to the global standard. the commission should monitor developments closely and keep it informed of the risks of loopholes and other problems. it must be clear that this reporting is not only done in a narrow circle in the council, but is also shared with parliament and the wider public. if deficiencies are detected, any measures must be proposed and taken as soon as possible. the transition to the global standard for the automatic exchange of account information is an excellent opportunity, which must be exploited in an excellent way.
mr president, i would like to convey my thanks to the rapporteur and say to mr helmer and all the others behind their borders: ‘welcome to the 20th century’. when you have arrived there, you will get the information that the rest of us are in the 21st century, where there are no borders for mobile telephones or the internet. that is why it is important and a step forward with this package, because now we are clarifying that we need a european telecom market. we are taking away some of the hindrances, but we need to do more. one of the things we need to do more is more spectrum, and i think it is important to go ahead and open up the 700 megahertz band, because that will be a precondition for europe to have the best capacities and higher speeds. to those who are advocating some sort of doctrinaire net neutrality, i would say that in reality you are hindering and blocking the emergence of new services, because you say that different services are not to be allowed to have different preconditions. we shall not discriminate and block, but we shall open up. what you are saying is not net neutrality, it is anti-net; it is not internet, because a growing and potential internet is open for suppliers and for users: that is the future. we need to go further ahead, but this is an important step forward.
‒ the european parliament voted today on a new eu law banning extra roaming charges for mobile phone use in other eu countries. this is welcome news for the citizens of europe, who will no longer be faced with skyrocketing bills just because they happen to cross the border between two member states. the new telecoms package also contains rules on high-speed internet access and so-called net neutrality (open internet). the law ensures that we will not have internet at two speeds (blocking or slowing down the internet for certain content is prohibited) and thus forces internet providers to treat traffic on equal terms. i voted for the package as a whole because it takes account of the citizens' right to an open and free internet, while preserving the possibility for certain services (e.g. alarm services) to be given priority as long as they are not provided at the expense of the quality of the open network.
‒ i voted in favour of the amendments safeguarding net neutrality and against the proposal because i am concerned that net neutrality risks being undermined by this decision. i'm a champion of an open internet where businesses and people are treated equally.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of this agreement with the member states in the council. it is gratifying that we have finally succeeded in abolishing roaming charges within the eu, although it is later than i would have liked. i would also have liked to see much stronger writing on net neutrality, an issue that i, many colleagues in the alde group and my predecessors as centre party mps fought for in the negotiations. i regret that, despite the pressure, we were unable to put more pressure on the member states. i do not think it would be responsible to vote for the amendments tabled for the plenary vote on that subject, because it would risk the whole proposal being rejected. we would then be back to square one, and i do not think the countries would have made any more concessions in the new negotiations.
‒ i voted in favour of the report. in a previous calculation commissioned by the commission, the annual losses from tax evasion in the eu member states were estimated at sek 9 000 billion. large companies in particular account for the majority of tax evasion. the eu budget deficit of €9 trillion is compared to the eu countries' total budget deficit in 2012 of about €4 trillion, or just half of what tax evasion costs. it is therefore good that the commission intends to tighten up legislation and the mandatory automatic exchange of information in taxation matters. the eu country of luxembourg has for several years been particularly distinguished for having organised its tax legislation in such a way as to help large companies avoid tax in other eu countries. i note that the prime minister of luxembourg, at the time when the country was tailoring tax avoidance laws for large companies, was jean-claude juncker. today he is president of the european commission and will therefore act against the very behaviour he himself engaged in a few years ago. it will therefore be important to keep a close eye on the commission. in the vote on the report i voted for the proposal that member states should send information to the commission and other member states about new proposals for their own taxation laws. the purpose of this exchange of information shall be to make tax evasion by large companies more difficult, and preferably impossible.
‒ the resolution on vehicle test methods was prompted by the systematic cheating of emissions values by the volkswagen group. i have voted against my own epp group on several occasions, including on the issue of transparency and public access to information on testing methods. i also voted in favour of requests that companies which have cheated be required to repay any subsidies and aid received on the basis of alleged environmental performance. this money is to be regarded as taxpayers' money and the fraud that has taken place has also distorted competition. i voted for the source code to be made available to the commission and the member states' authorities, in order to detect any manipulation devices. of course, this must be shrouded in strict secrecy to avoid leaks and industrial espionage. i voted against the creation of a federal european control authority, because the shortcomings of individual national authorities cannot be attributed to the failure of national controls as a whole.
in writing. ‒ i think it is good and important that parliament clearly signals against deliberate environmental fraud and against too poor testing methods for car emissions. european consumers must be able to trust that the car they buy actually delivers on the emission levels promised. that is why i voted in favour of the proposal.

for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. today's vote is the result of work and negotiations, which have taken a lot of time and a lot of meetings. i would therefore like to thank james nicholson for his great contribution to the work, especially in view of the fact that the question of novel foods evokes so much fear and emotion. during the work, we were told what would happen if we accepted new foods. we would be eating kilos of meat from cloned animals, we would be eating live insects, we would be poisoned by nanomaterials, and so on and so forth. so it feels quite good to be able to state the obvious, namely: no, it will not happen. if you press the yes button, very few things will happen, very few unpleasant things. you will not eat cloned animals, you will not eat food that is not approved and tested, and you will not lose your tongue if you taste some new exotic fruit. i promise you that the sun will rise again tomorrow and that we will have people out there who think that politicians don't know anything about it - i can guarantee that. what will change is that experts will tomorrow decide unanimously across the eu whether novel foods are approved and safe. it will be decided by science, not by emotion. we will have a predictable eu system that reduces the time and procedure for approval. one more thing. some of us will even try a new exotic fruit, but that is not mandatory, it can be avoided. i would like to end by thanking all those who will be pressing the "yes" button. thank you for conquering fear, thank you for treating new foods the same way you treat new medicines, and thank you for showing that you are not afraid. we in the alde group would like to see ourselves as such people and will therefore support the proposal.

in writing. – i welcome the adoption of the paragraph on a roadmap for a single seat for the european parliament, and the fact that parliament has reiterated its call for a single seat. we need to be able to decide when and where to meet. this is an important step towards a more efficient parliament. i am the chair of the single seat steering group because i am committed to creating a more effective parliament. european citizens are asking for this. they do not understand why, or accept that, we waste eur 114 million and 19 000 tons of co2 every year on commuting between brussels and strasbourg. a majority of this parliament has repeatedly voted in favour of the savings of a single seat. this is a vote in favour of a more cost-efficient parliament. we stand ready to pursue a constructive dialogue with all those who want to find viable solutions for all the european institutions’ seats. i therefore encourage guy verhofstadt to bring this forward in his ongoing report in the committee on constitutional affairs, in line with the decision of the conference of presidents of 11 february 2015. the deadlock must be broken. now it is time for the member states to take shared responsibility and to act.

‒ today the european parliament voted on a report on the use of genetically modified food and feed (gmos). the commission's proposal to allow individual member states to ban gmos was rejected by a largely unanimous environment committee for two main reasons. firstly, there was concern that the proposal would lead to a dismantling of the internal market and cause trade-related problems with the outside world. secondly, the proposal was seen as a way of back-channeling the integration of gmos into the internal market. i therefore voted in favour of the environment committee's line of rejecting the proposal, guided by the precautionary principle and the expectation of clear impact assessments from the commission.
in writing. i voted in favour of the report. genetically modified organisms (gmos) are controversial and face strong opposition both in sweden and in the eu. the left party is critical of gmos and believes that the precautionary principle should be safeguarded in the first place. this was the second time the commission had put forward a proposal on gmos, and although the commission had substantially improved them and given member states more scope to say no to gmos, both the left group, the greens and the socialist group agreed that stronger proposals were needed as more loopholes remained. that is why i voted to withdraw the motion, but also to support the resolution at the end.
‒ i voted to reject the proposal as a whole, as it does not take into account existing research and science. the proposal would also constitute a direct obstacle to the single internal market in the eu. if the proposal were to become a reality, it would also severely restrict trade with the outside world without any factual basis. against this background, i welcome the rejection of the proposal.
in writing. ‒ i think it is important that there is a good scientific basis for decisions and that authorities with expertise, such as efsa, can play an important role. that is why i voted against the motion.
‒ the european parliament voted today on a report on 'new foods'. the aim is to facilitate the entry of foodstuffs with altered molecular structure, consisting of micro-organisms, fungi or algae, etc., onto the european market. i voted for the compromise that was voted through in the committee on the environment, as i want to see new legislation in place as soon as possible. i also welcome the fact that this compromise contains transitional provisions for food from cloned animals.
‒ i have voted against the report. new food is a positive thing in itself, but there are too many shortcomings in this regulation. the report covers both novel foods, cloned animals and nanomaterials and their use in the food sector. the final proposal lacks sufficient safeguards for the precautionary principle and thus fails to put consumers and food safety first. our more stringent amendments were voted down. finally, it is important to invoke the principle of subsidiarity. the member states which do not want this type of product should have the right to regulate their own market. however, it is doubtful how this will be followed up when the eu is now voting on a regulation that puts the main power at eu level. in view of this, i have decided to vote against the report.
in writing. - we voted for the agreement with the council. the legislation means we'll be able to approve new foods more quickly. this is a positive development, especially since the existing legislation is an obstacle to food production. the law requires labelling and thus traceability of cloning, which could conflict with wto agreements and create other trade-related problems. it is unrealistic to require all our trading partners to introduce traceability systems for cloned animals. despite this objection, we voted in favour of the proposal as it is important to have a new system for the speedy approval of novel foods in place.
‒ a prerequisite for the development of europe's food sector is the existence of a regulatory framework in place that allows for the approval and launch of new innovative products on the common market. the proposal provides for a fully harmonised eu-wide procedure for the safety evaluation and authorisation of innovative food products. in addition, the european food safety authority (efsa) shall carry out an evaluation in all cases where food may have an effect on human health. the proposal provides the conditions for development and consumer safety. i have therefore voted in favour of this proposal.
in writing. - we have voted in favour of a council agreement on so-called new foods. the legislation means we'll be able to approve new foods more quickly. this is a positive development, especially since the existing legislation is an obstacle to food production. the law requires labelling and thus traceability of cloning, which could conflict with wto agreements and create other trade-related problems. it is unrealistic to require all our trading partners to introduce traceability systems for cloned animals. despite this objection, we voted in favour of the proposal as it is important to have a new system for the speedy approval of novel foods in place.
‒ when the european parliament voted today on the so-called 'co-decision' directive on national emission ceilings for sulphur, nitrogen and other combustible gases, i voted in favour of the commission's proposal. this proposal will reduce emissions by 70% of what is technically possible by 2030. this will result in better air quality, which will have positive effects on both human health and the environment. i voted against the requests which wanted to go further than the commission because, unlike the commission's proposal, they do not have an impact assessment.
in writing. ‒ i voted for an ambitious air pollution policy and i am glad that this line was heard in the european parliament. the strict limit values adopted by 2030 are estimated to be able to halve the number of premature deaths caused by air pollution each year, which currently stands at around 400 000 in the eu. air pollution causes ten times more deaths than road accidents, and it is time for eu member states to do more to tackle the problem.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of the report on the future revision of the european citizens’ initiative (eci). we need to address the challenges that citizens have encountered since the adoption of the regulation in 2011. cutting red tape and increasing citizens’ access to information about the eci are crucial in terms of raising awareness and encouraging more citizens to get involved. eu offices in the member states should be used more to inform citizens about this great opportunity for them. the reasons supplied by the commission for refusing citizens’ initiatives should be detailed and clear. organisers should be better informed about their rights after a refusal and about which parts of their initiatives they may correct. citizens should be encouraged, not demotivated. i am glad that, as representatives of european citizens, we showed a strong commitment to review the citizens’ initiative regulation so as to ensure that citizens will fully enjoy this new right.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would also like to thank the whole team of colleagues, the rapporteur, the commission and of course the presidency, which is unfortunately absent from the house today. we have all worked very well and constructively over the past few years. sometimes there have been tough, sometimes tough, discussions between us. however, we are now at the finish line, and i think we should all be happy about what has happened. the challenge now will be to strengthen cooperation, to exchange experiences and best practices between member states and the various law enforcement authorities across our union. of course, i regret what we as liberals had as a guiding light, namely the merger of cepol and europol. it would have brought positive synergies, and it would have saved a lot of money for our taxpayers across the eu. but now it was not, and we are forced to realize that it was a majority that looked different. what is particularly positive now is that cepol now has in its job descriptions and conditions to strengthen respect for fundamental human rights and freedoms and to engage in crossborder law enforcement cooperation in a different and more up-to-date way. as we know, criminals do not know the borders between countries, and it is therefore very strange that our law enforcement agencies should not be able to work at least as effectively across borders as the criminal gangs they are supposed to fight. so now there are conditions. i think we're putting history behind us and realizing that we have everything to gain by working together even more. once again, i thank all concerned for the excellent cooperation. it's my pleasure, mr. president. (the rapporteur agrees to answer a question by means of a blue card (article 162 (8) of the rules of procedure))
blue-card answer.– as i put it in my speech, what we are seeing today is that criminal transnational gangs do not feel those borders between nations. i find it quite remarkable that there are still, in this globalised world where we are, and also in this union, people like yourself who believe that putting up fences, working nation by nation, would be efficient. i believe in an enhanced cooperation between nations in order to combat these transnational criminal gangs that are actually putting ordinary people in a very difficult situation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i'm torn as to whether cepol is or isn't. on the one hand, the situation in which the member states find themselves calls for increased police cooperation, and on the other hand, i realise that the main reason for this need is precisely the open borders imposed by the eu. this is a typical example of how the brains behind the eu project first create a problem and then present a solution which usually also has the effect of increasing the eu's power. in the situation we are in today, with a schengen regulation which allows people to travel freely, we see an increase in drugs, human smuggling and trafficking. how can cepol train our police when politicians can't even cope with the problems we see today and, above all, don't even want to acknowledge the cause of them? in order to make it easier for our police, it should be reasonable to allow member states to maintain their border guards.
for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. it is high time that we abandon the rhetoric and the eulogy and instead stand up and seriously defend the basic human rights, which mass surveillance is absolutely not a part of. there is no room for mass surveillance of citizens for liberals. the developments are worrying all over europe. we are actually seeing several steps backwards in terms of the very views on privacy and data protection. instead, the starting point seems to be increasingly based on the erroneous assumption that the more data we collect, the safer citizens become. nothing could be more wrong! my group deeply regrets that the council and the commission have consistently refused to answer parliament's questions, which we first raised over a year ago in the debate on mass surveillance. we are also very concerned about the legislation now being drawn up in several member states, where bulk storage of data is now becoming a practice. the uk passed a law in 2014 that now needs to be reviewed because the high court has declared it invalid. germany has just decided on new data retention legislation, and france has introduced the possibility of mass surveillance of citizens for its intelligence service. we are also deeply concerned about the dutch intelligence legislation. i would urge my liberal colleagues in the dutch government to take carefully into account yesterday's prakken d'oliveira judgment. that ruling prohibits the surveillance of lawyers' communications until an independent review is in place. the european court of justice's decision last week on safe harbor should also be a wake-up call to everyone. the judgment states that the us mass surveillance is not compatible with the laws and principles of the eu. we must now start sweeping up our own front door and not be dependent on the courts to correct the legislation which we should collectively take responsibility for ensuring that it actually puts fundamental rights at the centre. it is the duty of all of us not to protect governments but to protect the fundamental rights of citizens and to defend european values.
member of the commission.– madam president, i would like to thank members very much indeed for this very lively debate. it sends a very strong message – which was commented on before – that representatives of all parties are here discussing this issue. as stated in my introduction, the commission is aware of the different arguments for and against. i have spoken personally with many member states, asking them for their opinion, and i have to say that opinion is divided. it is not as coherent as members are being here. there are indeed many studies examining potential impacts on health, but there is still no really strong argument to be gained from them at this time. i agree that the challenge is greater than we are sometimes willing to admit. i hear what members are saying – honestly – but let me also stress that my strong motivation is to keep the single market alive and to have a unanimous decision on this issue at european level. so, at this stage, the commission is not considering changes to the relevant directive but, should new evidence emerge and a more systemic approach be put forward, we would be willing to reconsider that position.
member of the commission.– madam president and distinguished members of parliament, drones are a technology which has already changed the way we do things and which creates opportunities for new services and new applications. unmanned aircraft are able today to perform missions that are not conceivable or economically not feasible with manned aircraft, ranging from safety inspections – as already pointed out – and monitoring activities to precision farming operations or assessment of natural disasters. the range of drone applications for civilian use is developing fast, underlining the significant economic potential of this sector to contribute to growth and jobs in europe. everybody acknowledges the enormous potential of drone technologies, yet citizens are also raising concerns about drone operations: hence the challenge for us to come up with rules that strike the right balance between promoting this promising technology and adequately protecting our citizens. it is our conviction that only common european rules would provide a solid basis for promoting investment in, and facilitating the development of, drone technologies while duly protecting all our citizens. that is why the commission greatly welcomes the support for this approach in the foster report. i would really like to thank the rapporteur, ms foster, for her great work and her efforts in bringing together the views of different political groups in her very comprehensive report. the commission agrees with the report’s call for a clear, harmonised and proportionate eu regulatory framework, removing the current 150 kilogramme threshold separating eu and national competence. we need to go for common rules for all drones, regardless of weight. but please understand me, this does not mean that every drone operation requires heavy-handed regulation. the commission agrees with the report that we need to evolve towards an operation-centric approach to safety rules that are proportionate to the risk associated with a particular operation. these ideas are already being developed by the european aviation safety agency and the joint authorities for rulemaking on unmanned systems (jarus), where experts from the european member states play an active role together with experts from the us, china, japan, brazil, canada, and many more. the commission also supports the idea that safety rules could be supplemented by internal market or international commerce rules, using leaflets, for example, to make each drone user aware of possible risks. the commission is aware of the need to let drones operate further away from the ground station, in other words further than the pilot can see. if drones are allowed to operate over a longer distance in one mission, the cost of the operation will decrease drastically, and wider-range operations will encourage innovative businesses. and yes, we should find sufficient r&d funding to finance the missing technologies. while we support the development of drones, public acceptance of drone operations is key, and we therefore agree that adequate enforcement of safety and other existing legislation, for instance on privacy or data protection, is crucial. however, there are a number of other aspects related to drones, such as security, environmental protection and liability, and these also need to be addressed. let me conclude: the commission greatly appreciates this report on drones, which will significantly contribute to steering safety rules in the desired direction and to the commissionʼs ongoing work to lay down an enabling framework for drones. the topic has been thoroughly addressed in the aviation package, and i hope that we will be able to proceed swiftly in the near future.
the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has tabled an opinion. president. - the debate is closed. drones are becoming increasingly important in our society both commercially and for performing vital community services. a key use of drones is the monitoring of people, often without their knowledge. these days, anyone can buy a drone with a camera or other equipment. eu privacy and personal data protection legislation must be respected in a civilian drone market. this is non-negotiable and cannot be countered by commercial interests. the commission must carefully investigate the risks posed by a market for civilian drones and inform the public about them. the commission must also come up with a proposal for a regulatory framework that everyone can feel safe with. we also need to look at how intelligence services use drones and how we can ensure that our fundamental rights are not violated.
for the efdd group. president. - the debate is closed. drones are a growing business with a very high potential. in any case, i believe that clear rules are needed for this market to allow for further investment in technology. people are often skeptical of new ideas. just think of the infancy of flight -- most people dismissed the men who developed airplanes as crazy people. today, i think very few of us could imagine a world without the ability of flight to move quickly between different continents. a trip to the united states in the past, at least from my home country of sweden, was about a couple of weeks. technology is thus evolving and advancing all the time. the problem is that we often have difficulty in taking it in before we see the practical possibilities of it. the industry can therefore become very large in this market. it is large and can become even larger, but it also requires very clear rules of the game in the market, because no company would dare to invest a lot of money in a novelty without knowing what the rules of the game are. well, integrity is, as i said, a very sensitive issue. who wants a drone over their head when they're sitting in the garden drinking coffee? so this is an aspect that the legislators need to look at, so that privacy is protected. but i see great social opportunities in technology.
for the efdd group. president. - the debate is closed. ladies and gentlemen, tourism is an area which i believe the eu should keep its fingers very far away from. tourism is a national, a regional and, indeed, mostly a local issue in each member state. it is the unique characteristics of each country that attract tourism. in sweden, it is the beautiful nature with fresh air and great opportunities for silence and peace and quiet. in spain, for example, it's the sun and the heat. i do not want to see the bizarre ideas of the eu being incorporated into the tourism sector. i am sure that the vast majority of you in this house will vote in favour of the proposal. you have long since betrayed your countries and your constituents. you are giving up your countries' sovereignty and self-determination bit by bit on every issue without any qualms whatsoever. let me remind you that people have tried to build great empires before, but they have failed every time. it will fail this time too. member of the commission.– madam president, the commission wishes to thank the european parliament and the honourable member mr cramer for this question, which reflects the specific worries and expectations of a great number of european citizens. we fully support steps in the direction of global flight tracking, such as the ads-b (automatic dependent surveillance – broadcast) system, and we look forward to further steps being taken on the basis of the legislative work i outlined in my introductory remarks. i hope that this clarifies the efforts being made by the commission for the definition of commonly acceptable safe and cost-efficient solutions for global flight tracking. we look forward to close collaboration with parliament and the council for the timely adoption of the proposed specific legislation.

in writing. ‒ i fully stand for privacy and fundamental rights. mass surveillance programmes should be condemned and intelligence activities submitted to democratic oversight. however, i could not support the resolution today. it is not responsible to ask for the suspension of the tftp agreement, which is a proven tool to prevent terrorism and an important legal framework to protect european citizens’ data. asking that all legislation concerning data processing should stop or be revised after two years would create legal uncertainty, not protect citizens’ privacy. encryption, a useful tool, should not become the only solution to increase privacy online. law enforcement authorities should be able to access data, in defined conditions, to investigate and persecute crime. allowing greater eu it independence, thinking that this would protect more eu citizens online, would lead to fragmentation of the internet and protectionism. while it is important to adopt at national level legislation that takes into account the situation of whistle-blowers, asking to give protection to edward snowden and for a european programme of protection of whistle blowers is not the way forward. it is all about finding the right balances. we need to protect privacy and ensure security for citizens. and today, this balance was not reached.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the resolution on the electronic mass surveillance of eu citizens. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the application of the principle of proportionality. we have full respect for snowden's important disclosures and his status as a "whistleblower" but do not believe that elected officials should interfere or try to influence the work of the courts. we therefore voted against this amendment.
in writing. ‒ order is important in the eu's different economies, and we need control and coordination. although i voted in favour of the final vote, i would point out that there are many areas where the eu level should not decide: the commission's recommendations should not be binding, and the constitutional rules of each country must determine how national parliaments handle budgetary votes and the like.
in writing. - i've lowered my vote. many points in the resolution are positive and underline the importance of measures to reach the longterm unemployed and open up opportunities for them to re-enter the labour market. the resolution, however, makes no mention of the austerity policies pursued by the european union, which have in many cases been imposed on the member states. it is a fundamental cause of mass unemployment in the eu. to combat longterm unemployment and the crisis in europe, a new economic policy with massive public and environmentally friendly investments is needed. investments for the benefit of society are required not only in education, health and care but also in infrastructure, railways, public transport, energy-efficient housing and sustainable energy systems. since the resolution does not have the broader perspective, i choose to abstain.
in writing. ‒ for the centre party and the alde group, the fight against unemployment is one of the most important issues. more people in work means more people in power, and that also means a more stable economic situation in europe. the resolution was good overall, but i want to clarify two of my votes. firstly, i think it is important that the social partners play an active part in the fight against unemployment. secondly, i do not think that it is necessarily a good policy to give more financial support to public employment services. the centre party in sweden has put forward a number of proposals on how the employment services can be changed and reformed, in order to open up private employment services, among other things. i think that is a more important part of the fight against unemployment than focusing on the public employment services.
‒ i have decided to abstain from voting on this report. the report focuses on drones in civil aviation and highlights the importance of the protection of personal data, the right to privacy and other fundamental rights, which is good. however, i cannot support the paper which stresses that an integrated civil/military air traffic service will make it easier to integrate rpas into israeli airspace. i will never be able to support a report which legitimises drones for military purposes or israel's war against palestine.
‒ i have voted against this report. the report aims to establish a single european tourist agency to promote the european tourist industry. the report is both pro-federalist and harmonising and it proposes that new money be allocated to this area. i believe that spending more money on tourism at a time when welfare sectors are being cut is the wrong priority. these are the main reasons why i voted no.
mr president, i think it is important that the commission takes note of two things in its annual growth survey. one is the change in and the challenge of the global economy. when i listen to the debate in this house, it is as if the changes in the global economy are not there – we are maybe looking at the internal market but not at the external one – but the fact is that it is very much the increased supply, increased productivity and increased competition we see today in the global economy that is the challenge to the european economy. where we lack competitiveness we are also suffering increased deficits, and that will not help with the increased spending. and that is the other thing to take note of in the survey report: that wherever you are trying to perform a policy without considering the realities of the global economy, by leaving the rules of the stability pact you are undermining the national economies. that is a very clear and obvious pattern. where you have stabilised public finances and fiscal stability, you have in most parts of europe growth or growth has returned, but where you are trying to solve the problems by increased spending, you get increased taxes and increased deficits and less employment. that is the truth and we need to know that. that gives hope for europe, because then we see what to do; maybe less hope for socialists.

mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you, vice-president šefčovič, for being here to take the debate. i would like to begin by saying that for me it is so extremely clear that this is one of the areas where we really need more of the european union and where we need more of the market. there are some forces that criticize the eu and say that it is only a matter of the market. here we have the clear proof that in the field of energy we have no single market at all. if we are to cope with the climate, if we are to cope with jobs, if we are to cope with industry, if we are to cope with the economy and security, it is of the utmost importance that we have a common energy union which is also very, very market-based. if we look at the example of the eu, the north pole, which concerns the nordic countries and the baltic countries, we can see, for example, if we look at estonia, how a common market has benefited the economy, both for private households and for industry, and how costs have been reduced through more efficient use of energy. it is also based on getting more interconnectivity, which will enable us to ensure that the parts of the union where we have an energy surplus can compensate for the parts of the union where we have an energy deficit. it also means that we will have greater security throughout the european union. i would urge the house not to get too caught up in the details of what we have on this market since. now we're going to create the system, the security, the rules and the market. then we have other instruments, such as the ets, to ensure that we are steering in the direction we want to go. but we will not be able to cope with jobs, the economy or the climate unless we have a common energy union that is strong, clear and market-based.
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you. i speak swedish, i'm going to. i want to thank the commissioner. this is a very ambitious and important work that you have started in the commission. i think you should be able to do that with even more confidence, because what has happened in the last few years in the energy market is that we have had a revolution. the prices of renewable energy, wind and solar have fallen dramatically and this is changing the whole energy market. in particular, if we implement what the commission wants to have a better interconnected energy system, where the whole of europe can transport energy from different places. this allows us to invest where it is most efficient and also to achieve competitive prices for the future. we must be able to stand up and speak about this as a future guarantee for industry in the future too. that is why i think we should dare to say, more clearly, that we must phase out coal. what i'm worried about is the elephant in the room. you have to have the courage to be clearer in that area, to gain confidence in the other.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is very welcome that we are now taking the step towards achieving an internal energy market while at the same time achieving a sustainable situation from both an environmental and a supply perspective. one of the key areas is energy efficiency for heating and cooling. the heating option that best complements energy efficiency efforts is district heating. larger incinerators have better purification and are more efficient than those you can have at home, so there are already gains to be made, but there are also huge amounts of energy from waste heat from, for example, industrial processes that can be used. this means less emissions, less imports and, moreover, an opportunity for industry to create an energy market that would otherwise have been wasted and thus a better competitive situation. i therefore hope that the commission will see district heating and cooling as an opportunity to strengthen the approach already taken to energy efficiency in buildings.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am positive that this framework is now finally coming into place. the consumer has long been a weak player in the insurance market. the abuses have been obvious. it has been all too common for consumers to be left with the wrong insurance product at a price that is far too high. the new rules will bring about marked improvements. consumers will be given clearer information, more sound advice and, in general, stronger protection. a particularly important provision is that the consumer should not be forced to buy such insurance as is offered in packages with another good or service. the product or service must be available for purchase without insurance. all of this is a valuable step in the right direction. what is now crucial is that the directive is properly implemented at national level. the change in the rules must be accompanied by a fundamental change in the market culture: the consumer must be put first.
‒ i voted against the second part of paragraph 47 because i believe that all children should have access to health care, education and childcare, but the way this is organised budgetarily in the different eu member states is different. in sweden, for example, this concerns primarily municipal and regional budgets. the member states should continue to be free to design this according to their systems and i therefore vote against all member states allocating a specific budget line to this issue.
‒ the resolution contains a number of deeply problematic points. among other things, i think it is completely wrong for the eu to advocate a statutory minimum wage, that is something for the member states to decide and in sweden wages should be set by the social partners. it is also wrong policy to advocate increased public spending and to suggest that the commission should refrain from recommending privatisations and more flexible working conditions. a number of member states, including sweden, are facing major structural challenges that require both more flexible working conditions and privatisations. i voted against these errors but i voted for this resolution. the reduction of child poverty is a very important issue and this report addresses, among other things, the need for strong protection systems to protect children from violence and the need for preventive work to reduce poverty. these are important issues for me and i have chosen to support them by voting in favour of the report. both the work to reduce child poverty and the work to make the labour market more flexible will continue and i look forward to being a part of that work.
in writing. more than one in four children in the eu is at risk of poverty or social exclusion today. between 2008 and 2012, the number of children at risk of poverty or social exclusion in europe increased by almost one million, of which half a million increased between 2011 and 2012 alone. the consequences are many, for example, the increase in the proportion of children suffering from malnutrition, with the consequence that diseases that had disappeared from the eu (such as rickets) are reappearing. the fight against child poverty needs to be given greater political attention as a matter of urgency, also in order to enable the eu to achieve the europe 2020 target of reducing the number of poor by at least 20 million by 2020. we social democrats therefore welcome this own-initiative report. however, we would like to stress that access to education, health care and social security for children is primarily a matter for the member states and national welfare systems. at the same time, the eu can play a major role in supporting the work of the member states, in particular through the structural funds. the eu can also facilitate crossborder cooperation and develop common guidelines and objectives to combat child poverty. ensuring that the child perspective is included in all relevant policy-making processes, including at eu level, is also a successful way to prevent and combat child poverty.
‒ all children should have access to healthcare, education and childcare, but the way this is organised budgetarily differs from one eu member state to another. in sweden, for example, this concerns primarily municipal and regional budgets. the member states should continue to be free to design this according to their systems and i therefore vote against all member states allocating a specific budget line to this issue.

‒ the christian democrats are clear that the long-term ambition is to gradually reduce eu spending on agricultural, labour market, regional and industrial policies. as the position of the european parliament in the budgetary negotiations was the opposite, i voted against this earlier. however, i voted in favour of the agreement between parliament and the council because the eu needs a budget to function and because all the member states support the agreement.
‒ the agreement provides for a small increase in the budget due to increased efforts, in particular to deal with the current refugee situation, but also measures to boost growth in the eu. a further factor contributing to the additional need for budgetary resources is that many of the previously planned projects have started and need disbursements. i would have liked to have seen these changes managed by reallocation within the existing budget, but i have nevertheless chosen to vote for the final agreement.
‒ we voted for the eu budget for 2016 because it is obvious that the eu must have a budget. we voted against the demands made by the majority of parliament in the negotiations with the council. what has now been decided was the only option for a functioning eu. we welcome the fact that the agreed budget gives priority to the challenges posed by the refugee issue and to research and development. we regret that this is happening by increasing the budget as a whole and not by making savings commensurate with the priorities. in the long term, we want to reduce agricultural aid, industrial subsidies and those parts of regional policy which make well-functioning economies dependent on regional subsidies. the eu should give priority to the infrastructure of the single market, research and our long-term capacity to develop an effective foreign policy and jointly strengthen control of our borders. it should be done within the framework of the current long-term budget and based on respect for taxpayers' money and the strained budgetary situation of the member states.
