in writing. we swedish social democrats voted yes to the taxe report. we regard this as an offensive and partly visionary text which pushes the debate forward on issues of tax evasion and aggressive tax planning. all the recommendations may or may not be implemented in the near future, but it is useful to establish a clear and concrete agenda now. it is important to emphasise in this context that the tax rates themselves in this area must remain essentially a national matter. it must be left to the member states themselves to determine how high the tax rates need to be in order to ensure sufficient revenue in the public budgets.
in writing. - i have abstained from this report. radicalisation is an important issue but this report is being used to implement more surveillance of people and to empower internet providers to share information with europol and member states. in addition, there was far too much focus on young people being radicalised into islamist organisations without recognising the great dangers of right-wing radicalisation. some good things came through, though, like recognizing that social and economic factors contribute to radicalization and that religion itself is not the problem but misinterpretations of it.
‒ the report addresses a very topical issue, which is important for the safety and security of society, namely preventive measures to counter the radicalisation of young people in particular. however, this should not necessarily be confused with counter-terrorism measures, as these are people who have already been radicalised. i have voted for the report as a whole but have voted against certain parts on counter-terrorism which i think should be dealt with in a different context.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report on preventing the radicalisation and recruitment of eu citizens by terrorist organisations. we abstained on amendment no 50 because of the ambiguity of the wording. we support border checks at the eu's external borders to detect any suspected terrorists trying to enter the eu. however, these must never lead to the denial of entry at the eu's external borders to persons in need of protection, which would be contrary to eu law and international law. we supported that people who have fought for isis and who return should be checked and prosecuted if crimes have been committed. finally, the report also contained problematic writings on the role of internet companies in removing content 'contributing to the spread of violent extremism', which we did not support. we nevertheless voted in favour of the report in the final vote. the eu and its member states need to do more to prevent violent extremism.
‒ i do not believe that the resolution is the ultimate solution to the question of how we can prevent eu citizens from being radicalised and recruited by terrorist organisations. the resolution contains both positive and negative calls. the positive aspects include the non-association of terrorism with specific religions or nationalities, the need to ensure that data collection and other investigative measures are carried out in accordance with the fundamental values of eu law and international human rights standards, and the need to combat arms smuggling and illicit arms. i voted against the call to complete the eu passenger name record (pnr) directive. it is more important that the individual police authorities of the countries cooperate with each other and that we devote more resources to the ordinary police and security police work. the crucial work of de-radicalisation is carried out on the ground and requires the efforts of municipalities, authorities and civil society.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we have just voted on the strategic framework on health and safety at work. for sweden, for many years, not least through the efforts of the trade unions and through national political decisions, we have taken many important steps to protect workers, both in terms of occupational health and safety at work. the swedish model, which i defend and stand up for, i do not want to see watered down by the eu sticking its fingers in this area too. that is why i am voting against this proposal. the eu is constantly trying to get its fingers in more and more national affairs. unfortunately, it rarely gets better because a one-size-fits-all model is a complete failure. if you are a member of the eu, you should also understand the importance of taking responsibility for your workers and their safety, without the eu coming in with its thumbs up and pretending to understand better than the national governments do.
in writing. ‒ for us moderates, it is self-evident that the eu member states have adequate legislation to guarantee a good working environment for all those who work. each country has both a responsibility and an obligation to introduce adequate occupational safety legislation, covering everything from musculoskeletal disorders to workplace bullying. but there is no added value in more common eu legislation in this area. however, it is good that member states coordinate their policies and exchange experience with each other in order to improve national legislation. we have therefore voted in favour of the report, but against the demands for more eu legislation in this area.

for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i was part of the european parliament's election observation mission to myanmar, along with six other parliamentary colleagues. the impressions of this mission are positive. the quality of the voting process and the counting of votes was high. no serious irregularities were reported. voter turnout was estimated at about 80 percent. after the elections, the incumbent government has admitted defeat. opposition leader aung san suu kyi and her nld party won nearly 80 percent of the vote and negotiations are now under way to form a new government. with that said, it is essential to see the election in myanmar in its full political context. despite successful implementation and political progress in recent years, the country cannot yet be considered a democracy. the current constitution contains parts that are deeply problematic, such as the fact that myanmar's military power is guaranteed a quarter of the seats in parliament. aung san suu kyi is barred from holding the important post of president under another part of the constitution. in addition, human rights violations are a serious problem. the number of political prisoners is increasing, as is the discrimination against minorities. among the worst affected are the rohingya and other muslim groups, who have suffered severe abuses and persecution for a long time. the challenges facing the new parliament are therefore considerable. much political leadership and will to reconciliation will be required in myanmar's continued path towards stability and peace, as well as continued support from the european union.

the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. on 14 july this year, the current luxembourg presidency of the council gave a favourable opinion on the directive on the more equal representation of women on the boards of listed companies, thank you for that. but luxembourg is not alone in this, both the european parliament and the oecd now see the clear need for a directive. more equal representation on company boards is supported by both economic and feminist arguments. the oecd bases its position on the fact that companies benefit statistically from including more women on boardrooms in purely financial terms. aside from the economic, it's also high time that the glass ceilings and patriarchal networks that reproduce male boardrooms are undermined. since companies have had the opportunity to improve without much success, legislation must now be put in place.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have worked for women's rights all my life and i have always been in male-dominated contexts, even here in the european parliament, i might add. but i do not think that the commission's directive on women on boards is the best way forward for real equality. firstly, because i, as a liberal, believe that companies should be able to decide for themselves how they are to be governed and represented, and secondly because we must respect the right to property, which has been fundamental in every european democratic state. and the right to own is still fundamental. but above all because i think it's starting from the wrong end. if we want to achieve gender equality, we must have equal parental leave, good childcare in all our member states so that women and men can participate in working life on equal terms. today, far too many women give up their careers to become housewives. the eu, mr president, simply cannot afford to have the most highly educated housewives in the world. we must stop doing that and make sure that we work, both women and men.
mr president, it is with great distress that we have witnessed the constantly worsening state of democracy and rule of law in hungary over the past years. the freedom of the media has been curtailed through fidesz-controlled media supervisors who have brought about fear and self-censorship. the court system has been politicised and the freedom of minorities is being trampled on by the government. this week we saw the arrest of a number of opposition politicians in hungary, purely for having shared political criticism of fidesz policies on facebook. it was notable that, although these posts were shared by many individuals, the actions of the now fidesz-run judiciary of hungary targeted its attacks at active members of the political opposition. i am usually a big fan – a supporter even – of the commission, but i would say to the commissioner that on hungary, quite frankly, the commission is nothing but pathetic in your inaction. you have done absolutely nothing with regard to parliament’s resolution adopted in june. you only offer words and hot air and not a single concrete political action. you are supposed to be the guardians of the treaties, not the guardians of oppressive governments. what are you waiting for? does mr orbán have to turn into an exact copy of mr lukashenko before you wake up and smell the coffee? i find it appalling. your inaction leaves a lot to be desired by citizens out there. take action on this and do it now.
in writing. - i abstained in the vote. the fact that frontex has not had an individual complaint mechanism until now is remarkable, especially in view of the recurring reports of human rights violations coming from both amnesty and the un refugee agency. such a mechanism must be independent of frontex and the complainant has the right to remain anonymous and may be represented by a third party, such as iom, amnesty or the un refugee agency. results are highly questionable. an internal mechanism within frontex is not enough. however, we were told that the plaintiff may be represented by a third party. it is a small step in the right direction, but it is not enough to be a system that can be called legally secure.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i have to say, as the president has just said, that for the first time we have a global climate agreement on our hands, and that is something that must empower all of us who have been involved in working to find a solution to these difficult issues over the last ten to twenty years. there's a process for audits and follow-ups, and we have an agreement that says we have a target that is well below two degrees. it's a great success. the question is what this means for the european union. yes, some of the things are that we must speed up the work on creating a common energy market and speed up the work on the energy union. we need to end fossil fuel subsidies faster than we have thought of doing so before. we also need to put a price on coal and ensure that we have a functioning emissions trading system which is not yet working properly, and we need to concentrate even more on energy efficiency to be able to meet the climate targets. these are issues that are raised in the energy union, but also in my report which i have presented here, which deals with the interconnection of the electricity grids in europe. with a common electricity grid and a common market, we can achieve much of what we are striving for. we can get cheaper electricity, cheaper electricity production, cheaper electricity to consumers and to businesses in europe, and we can also get more renewable energy and more investment with a common electricity market that works, and get more investment in renewable electricity where it is most efficient. in places like the north sea, we can get more wind power produced. in spain, we can get more solar energy produced. with a larger, more coherent electricity grid and a common market, we can make this work together. the different renewable energy laws also link and balance each other better. we can therefore also more easily achieve the climate objectives we have now set ourselves. but we can also get a better competitiveness by cheaper electricity for european industry, and that is especially important in the present situation when we still have very high unemployment in many countries. if we can remove the energy islands that we still have in europe, for example in poland, in the united kingdom, in spain and on the iberian peninsula, then that will also contribute greatly to strengthening competitiveness and european industry. i don't think one goal is enough for the future. we need to complement this with regional targets to remove bottlenecks in the electricity networks and we also need to strengthen the regulatory authority acer with a better budget, more staff and with better possibilities to exercise its supervision and control. finally, we also need to make greater efforts to introduce the best available technology (bat) when investing in new electricity grids. this is because we are reducing conflicts with the local population and are making investments more rapidly.
i think that we are in agreement. i want us to use the 10% target and that is important, but i also think that there are other, regional, complementary targets which are also important if we are to have a functioning common electricity market, such as within some countries, which is needed to strengthen the electricity grid, but also between some countries which already have other problems. so to remove the bottlenecks and achieve a common electricity market, we also need complementary targets, not just the 10% target.
blue-card question.– i would like to emphasise one thing that is important in this debate. the criticism often comes from countries like poland and the united kingdom. however, when we look at electricity prices in europe, the countries that have the highest prices are britain and poland – and also spain sometimes. this is because they are energy islands. the countries that have good connections with the other countries around have low prices and better competition for their industry.
blue-card question.– i agree with almost everything you say, but it is also important to have evidence-based checks on what you are doing, because we are dealing with taxpayers’ money. we should only use taxpayers’ money in as effective a way as possible. it is perhaps not the most efficient thing to lay a new cable from cyprus to greece to solve this problem. maybe it is better to invest in electricity and renewables in cyprus itself, for example.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would also like to thank my shadow rapporteurs and those who have worked on the energy union and also thank you for the discussion with commissioner cañete, because it has been a very interesting and important debate that we have had here today, and i want everyone to know that we have big tasks ahead of us. we are talking about very serious, major climate change that is coming and that will change our planet. we still have a huge dependence on fossil fuels. we import the majority of our energy into the european union. we have very high prices and low competitiveness in several countries because of too high energy prices. many also have regulated prices and therefore no functioning market, but there are two things that make me more optimistic about the future than in the past. on the one hand, the dramatic fall in renewable energy prices in recent years gives us a much greater opportunity to make meaningful large-scale investments in renewable energy and to bring more renewable energy into the energy mix. the second thing is what happened this weekend, when we got a climate agreement that actually makes all the countries on the planet stand behind a work to reach the less than two degrees target. we have great opportunities, also by investing in a common electricity market in europe, to regain more competitiveness, to bring in more renewable energy and, moreover, to bring cheaper energy to consumers.
rapporteur.– mr president, i have had the privilege of being your rapporteur on the trademarks package since the spring of 2012. i would like to take this opportunity to thank all colleagues and their staff, both in parliament and the commission. i would also like to warmly thank the many presidencies that have worked on this dossier, and perhaps most importantly the italian and the latvian presidencies, which provided excellent work in the most critical stages of the negotiations. i would like to draw your attention to the achievements on this file. the reforms agreed in trialogues will ensure that companies in europe have faster and cheaper access to trademarks, both at national and european levels, without compromising on the quality of the decisions. administrative procedures have been streamlined according to best practices, and we will see an increased harmonisation of the national systems along the lines of best practices. on substantive trademark law, we have modernised a number of provisions in view of technological developments. we have also managed to include important safeguards to protect freedom of expression by limiting the possibility of rightholders to bring legal actions against people using trademarks for reasons, for example, of parody or artistic expression. for this, i would like to thank an old colleague from sweden who was with us but who is not any more. he drew our attention to this. some of you might have received letters during the week from ngos expressing concerns on the issue of transit of generic medicines to developing countries. i would very much like to underline the position and the fact: i can assure everyone that there will in no case be any problems for the transit of generic medicines to developing countries as a result of this legislation. throughout my life i have fought for development, and i would never, ever allow a law bearing my name to hinder people in developing countries from getting access to medicines. that is for sure. the ambition has been shared by the entire parliament negotiating team. we have been united in this, and together we managed to include very strong safeguards. it should also be noted that the problems that have occurred in past times with regard to the transit of medicines have exclusively related to patents, and not trademarks. i am proud today of the balance that we have achieved between fighting organised crime and the dangerous trafficking of counterfeit products, and protecting the interests of legitimate trade. i would like to stress that there is no risk of confusion between generic medicines and counterfeit, fake medicines. any holder of a european trademark trying to incorrectly stop goods in transit which are not breaching the rights is also liable for damages in accordance with the eu customs regulations. these rules will give our customs officers the right to seize counterfeit fake products transiting through europe. now, in europe, we will be able to help developing countries in an important struggle against organised crime, which preys on the most vulnerable of us. this is something that we are all united in. i will come back later, but i look forward to the debate on this item.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking rapporteur wikström and the other shadow rapporteurs. this was the first case in which i participated in a tripartite negotiation and i hope i have learned a great deal from this very fascinating work. there is a lot of good in this report. the system gets simpler, it gets more flexible, it partially solves the problem that khim managed to raise a huge amount of money by distributing a huge amount of brands, so there's a lot of good that's being achieved. as regards the question of freedom of expression and the question of generic medicines, we greens were the most important issues in this report, and as regards generic medicines, i am unfortunately not as confident as the other speakers that we have gone all the way. it was an issue that we had to spend a lot of time fighting against the council, and there is now a concern in civil society. we have just received a letter from the accessibility campaign of doctors without borders and 49 other organisations expressing concern that the right to generic medicines is not sufficiently guaranteed in this proposal. there is a concern, a risk, that generic medicines will be mistakenly stopped in eu ports. we have tried to minimise that risk, but we in the green group would like to say that we have not gone far enough on this front. we fought for more, and i'd like to say we did our best. the greens will vote no, because this question of generic medicines is the most important for us in this case.
rapporteur.– mr president, let me finally mention a few issues related to the governance and finance issues. parliament has managed a number of important victories here as well; transparency has been increased in all areas of the agency, and parliament will have a full seat on the board of the agency. the agency, previously known as the office for the harmonisation of the internal market, or ohim, will change its name to the european intellectual property office (eu ipo). we have succeeded in lowering the fees as much as possible without interfering with the national systems, and will increase the funding dedicated to cooperation projects between the eu agency and national agencies, which will lead to soft harmonisation as well as capacity-building in the national offices. all in all, this is a very large and complex dossier that will now come to an end and, as i have said many times throughout these years: this is not a revolution, it is a modernisation which was needed on the existing eu trade mark system. i am convinced that this dossier will lead to the aim that we all share; namely to foster growth and prosperity for europe, so badly needed these days. i trust that colleagues will support the agreement that we have reached and i thank you all again for your support and for very fruitful, and sometimes quite lively, work and discussions on this dossier. i wish you all the best.
in writing. - i voted yes to the report. i would like to emphasize that i am opposed to giving the eu taxation rights. but in this case it is a question of reforming an already existing system of payments by the member states for their vat and gni-based contributions to the union budget. in 2014, the current system's ambiguities caused serious problems, as several member states paid incorrect amounts, either too much or too little. the reforms are intended to simplify and clarify the calculation and payment methods so that similar problems can be avoided in the future. that is why i voted yes.

‒ today we voted on financial aid from the so-called globalisation fund to people who have become unemployed after european companies have closed down. i am consistently voting no to these unemployment benefits for two reasons: first, labour market policies and financial support for the unemployed should be decided on, and financed, at national level not at eu level. secondly, the fund's basic idea is that globalisation is something negative - which is not true. globalization and free trade lead to growth and peace.
in writing. ‒ we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we regard as an expression of protectionism. we have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the exclusion that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in the light of the swedish national audit office's analysis, which suggests that the fund does indeed have a negative impact on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its legitimacy and should not be used any more.
‒ i have voted against the report. firstly, this is an enlargement of the eu, and in some environmental cases that may be fine, but the eu's energy union aims to push electricity prices, create an internal market and transfer expertise from the member states to the eu. in addition, it is in favour of shale gas extraction and continued subsidies for nuclear power. i will never support initiatives that look only at the market without raising the need for energy efficiency and renewable energy.
‒ this report is an important position taken by the european parliament, which calls for energy diversification, completion of the internal energy market, improved energy efficiency and energy savings, increased use of renewable energy sources, more active use of european energy resources, better interconnections between member states and the renovation of infrastructure networks and buildings. access to safe and environmentally friendly energy is one of the key issues for europe's future. we need an energy system that promotes renewable energy and makes the eu less dependent on imports from uncertain partners.
‒ today the european parliament voted on the eu's microcredit facility for micro-enterprises. the vote was on parliament's observations on the commission's report on the implementation of the micro-credit system. these loans are intended to serve as a means of helping the long-term unemployed, young people, refugees and other groups to enter the labour market by offering micro-credit when they start a business. it is basically a good idea, but it is not something that should be dealt with at european level. member states should be responsible for their own labour market and social policy. since both the commission's report and parliament's record of it were characterized by a contrary assessment, i chose to vote against.
‒ this report dealt with the financing of start-ups under the european progress microfinance facility. the instrument is not working optimally today and the changes highlighted in the report, including those aimed at making it easier for people far from the labour market to access these credits, are good.
in writing. ‒ we moderates are sceptical about the eu having a microcredit facility. but it is important that the european parliament makes reasonable and balanced comments on how the decision is implemented once it has been taken. that is why we voted in favour of the report.
on behalf of the alde group.– madam president, i would like to thank the commission today for its – quite modest – proposals on strengthening the european capacity to control our common borders. i am convinced that the proposals do not go far enough and still need to be significantly strengthened. knowing that there are member states in the council that still consider that these proposals go too far is to me quite depressing, to tell the truth. i sometimes wonder if governments of member states are living in a parallel universe. how else could they complain constantly about the lack of control of our common borders and then oppose measures to secure exactly this once they have been presented? this to me is utterly absurd. through schengen we have agreed to have one external border of the eu and no internal borders, which means that greece’s borders with turkey are as much swedish borders as are sweden’s borders with norway. how member states can argue that member states should police their own borders is simply absurd. imagine the united states with fifty sets of coastguards and border guards, where the us coast guard does not have the right to have its own equipment, but has to ask if it may kindly borrow that of the state for a while. this is an absurdity, and yet in europe it is upheld by governments of member states as the only logical solution. anyone who suggests that maybe a common border needs common border protection is portrayed as some kind of crazy federalist. europe needs today a single border control agency and this should become an exclusive competence of the eu. i wish that member states would stop worrying so much about their own bureaucracies and start, for once, to think about citizens and security.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. when i read this document, which i have learned about on the internet, i felt that the extreme right was on our backs. but instead of taking them on, you turned around and embraced them. you chose to close the borders because that's what the consequences are. a frontex superpower with extraordinary powers at all levels: patrolling, deporting, giving orders to member states, acting in third countries to stop people fleeing. not a word about the people who are fleeing, or how this border control is linked to our international commitments to provide protection for people fleeing, or how europe should take its responsibility. ladies and gentlemen, the eu does not depend on schengen or whether we show id cards or not. the legitimacy of the eu depends on whether we can take responsibility for receiving people who are fleeing, and therefore the legitimacy of the eu now hangs on a very thin line. when things get tough, when some countries fall apart, what do you do? then you fall back on the only common denominator; apparently, we should close the borders. we have no legal routes, we have no shared responsibility, border controls are confused with terrorism and refugees are defined as risks. we should have common border controls if we can agree at the same time on how to take in refugees together. because otherwise there is nothing to be together or to defend with external borders. then it was fake solidarity, then it was fake tears, because when the flight was at its peak, the eu closed its borders. this is unacceptable. they're going their way.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i think i have to say that the eu is going overboard here and sailing in the wrong direction. this will lead to even greater supranationality, whereby the member states are now deprived of their sovereignty to decide and determine their own border protection on a voluntary basis and to contribute to the protection of the external borders. this is explained by the recent exceptional situation of illegal migrants, weapons and terrorists crossing the mediterranean. this should have been understood long, long ago. we sweden democrats want to see intergovernmental cooperation in the protection of our external borders, where frontex's main task is to guard our external borders and the member states are to participate on a voluntary basis, and nothing else. if the commission, with the support of france and germany, tries to do this, i believe that brexit will soon be followed by svexit.
we obviously need to know who comes to europe and to control our common borders better. i therefore welcome the commission package to upgrade frontex’s role in managing external borders and enforcing solidarity. a frontex plus should support member states registering migrants, take fingerprints, assist in relocation return, check biometric passports, update databases and share information. but both the control and coastguards should remain a national competence. therefore, commissioner avramopoulos, we would welcome more clarification on your proposal. what about command and control – division of tasks between national border guards and european teams? who would trigger the intervention of the joint european teams? eu action should always aim at member states taking their responsibility in applying eu law, not at replacing member states’ responsibility to build up the national capability, and not doing the gap-filling of inadequate implementation of eu law. to conclude, controlling our border better is not closing our border: it is a must to make sure that freedom of movement within schengen will be ensured. it is a must to make sure that refugees are welcome in an ordered manner and for our security. ‒ i voted in favour of the report. the report welcomes the decision of the un secretary-general to convene the first world summit on humanitarian issues. there is a lot of good writing, including on gender equality and violence against women, and guarantees for women and girls to have access to sexual and reproductive health, including safe abortion, in the context of humanitarian crises. although i voted in favour of the report as a whole, i do not support the proposals to expand the opportunities for business and the private sector in development cooperation and to make it a key player in achieving the new development goals. that's not the way forward.
in writing. - i've put my vote down. the base metal industry is very important for europe and especially important for sweden. many industrial jobs have disappeared or moved outside the eu since 2008, and the sector is subject to fierce competition. it is important to preserve industrial work in sweden and europe. the report criticises in particular taxes and laws which are designed to protect workers and the environment, which is not the right way to go. we want to see an environmentally and socially sustainable base metal industry. although the report stresses the importance of energy efficiency and recycling, it does not go far enough in terms of the environment. that's why i chose to abstain.
‒ this report is less about the challenges of the base metals sector and more about the eu emissions trading system (eu ets). as the european parliament has just started work on the eu ets bill that the commission presented in july, it is very unfortunate that we are already locking positions without proper discussion, where they do not belong. we have therefore consistently voted against all the texts which go into detail about the eu ets and the co2 adjustment at the borders, whatever our opinion on the content itself, and voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. we have voted against a number of proposals which we consider to pre-empt the process already under way of considering the reform of the eu emissions trading system (ets). we share the rapporteur's concern about the development of the european base metals industry, and several of these proposals are constructive, progressive proposals which we would normally have supported. however, it is problematic that this report takes a far-reaching position on the ets, which risks prejudging the important reform of the ets, which is a system that affects many more sectors than the base metals industry and covers over 13000 installations of different nature and with different needs. we have therefore taken a restrictive line in the vote on this own-initiative report on the ets, but have chosen to support the report as a whole, since it concerns an entire industry which is currently in a very difficult situation.
‒ today parliament voted on the situation in hungary. my group, the epp, tabled its own motion for a resolution, which i certainly do not object to in substance, but which does not go far enough. the s&d, alde, gue and greens, for their part, tabled a much stronger motion for a resolution. their motion for a resolution is more characterized by an awareness of the serious developments in hungary. however, i do not agree that the so-called article 7 procedure, as proposed in this resolution, is the right way to go at this stage. instead, the commission's scrutiny of hungary should be broadened and deepened - a view which i also underlined by supporting paragraph h of the resolution. in the final votes, after a unanimous assessment, i decided to abstain both in the vote on the epp resolution and the joint resolution of the aforementioned groups.
mr president, i would like to thank you. some 200,000 migrants are expected to arrive in sweden this year. the welfare state and a government paralysed by naivety are no longer able to cope with the situation. camping is the latest invention. camping out! in our nordic climate, where minus degrees and snow are a common ingredient, we offer camping. a mass immigration of biblical proportions with no control whatsoever over who we let in and where the vast majority are economic migrants who have paid very large sums to get into europe. those who are most in need and need of help, we leave them there for the benefit of those who have been able to pay to come here. is it democracy to leave the weakest behind? those who really need our help in the areas close to the hotspots of the conflict. moreover, should not human rights also include the right to feel safe and secure in one's own country? unfortunately, without checks and border controls, this can no longer be guaranteed, as we have seen with the recent terrorist attacks.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, the debate today is a very good opportunity to start putting facts back in a debate that is getting more and more surrealistic by the day. the most flagrant example is the proposal made by the president of the european council, mr tusk, who put forward the idea that all asylum seekers should be detained for 18 months to screen if they are potential terrorists. the most bizarre thing is that he does not seem to even realise that this would be completely against existing eu legislation. let me say it again: the president of the european council is proposing that all persons seeking refuge in europe, fleeing war and persecution, from the smallest baby to the oldest man or woman, should be put in prison-like camps for a year and a half. mr president, this equals the darkest periods in european history that we do not want wish to see again. i think that it is shocking to the point that i personally think that mr tusk should be asked to publicly apologise for this. in the media today we hear talk about refugees illegally entering the union. this is a narrative that is not only dangerous, it is incorrect. it is not in any way illegal to enter a country in order to ask for asylum. this is a recognised human right in all of the eu’s member states. now, if we were to work together we would be able to offer shelter to those in need, but instead of that, rampant nationalism has taken over the public debate. this crisis has not been caused by refugees or the european institutions; it has been caused by nationalist governments and populists that are actively preventing a european solution to the situation. it is high time that we realised that the best way to solve the situation is to do it together. (applause) (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer.– my proposal is very simple and it goes for a large majority of the libe committee, of which i am coordinator. it is a common responsibility sharing, a distribution key to member states that is based on quantitative data such as population, gdp, employment rate, etc. it should also be taken into account which countries did the most for refugees up until now – like sweden, germany, austria and others. it is high time that the countries that did not participate stood up and took full responsibility because today there is a situation we have to handle. tomorrow it will be a different situation and we will all be stronger together.
mr president, i would like to thank you. together with a gue/ngl delegation, we went to the balkans and met with refugees. we met with organizations, and we also met with border guards. clearly, refugees have gone through more than any human should have to go through. they have the right to seek asylum and they have the right to a dignified reception here in europe. they're not illegal, and they shouldn't be locked up, and they shouldn't be subjected to violence. yet we have documented the brutality and confinement of refugees in turkey. we have an amnesty report on that. we also have reports from hungary that they lock up asylum seekers so that the asylum seekers themselves think that they have been in prison if they come from there. what refugees and medical staff tell us about police brutality and robbery in bulgaria is a reality. my question to the commission and the council is: what are you going to do to stop this? is it ok for the police of a member state to rob and use violence against refugees? how will you demand that turkey stop its violence against refugees, or will you continue to turn your back on them? are you going to ask the commission to ask member states to stop dublin transfers to hungary and bulgaria where refugees have been subjected to violence? i want concrete answers, because if you do nothing, then we who should be providing protection, instead of providing protection, are allowing the continuation of the violence and the trauma that no one should have to experience.
mr president, i would like to thank you. of course, detention and coercion should be avoided as far as possible. however, we should be aware that a large proportion of those who come to europe seeking asylum do not have valid reasons for seeking asylum. the only chance these people have of staying in europe is if they manage to cheat their way in, for example by refusing to leave fingerprints or otherwise sabotaging the asylum process. then there are those who have been denied asylum but still refuse to respect that and instead choose to become illegal immigrants. we must be able to take these people away from them and also use force to send them back. in both of these cases, we need to be able to use an appropriate level of coercion to get these people to respect our asylum process and the decisions that are made. the truth is that a large proportion of migrants cheat and exploit our systems. we need the tools to deal with these cheaters. the use of force in some cases must be part of that toolbox, or we will be inundated by these cheaters.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by making a statement. no human is illegal. the debate on refugee policy taking place in the eu is not conducive to our view of other people. the statements that we must strengthen the borders and that the eu must put a ceiling on the number of people fleeing, and that we are talking about people fleeing in volumes, easily make them lose their human dignity in the eyes of the public. we have a responsibility to lead, not to sit in the affectionate seat. it affects the way refugees are treated, especially in border areas. we know that there are cases where people who are fleeing are treated like criminals and cattle at border crossings. it's a disgrace to the eu and it's also illegal. every country is obliged to respect the human rights of all individuals on its territory, and it is illegal to deprive people of their liberty without cause. it is illegal to use violence against people who want to exercise their right to seek asylum. it's illegal not to treat people like people. the eu has a great responsibility in this matter. we have a common asylum policy and the commission must ensure that countries comply with this legislation. there is a need for effective and independent monitoring of national and eu border control authorities, which should be proactive and deal with complaints. eu authorities must prioritise respect for human rights in the workplace and disseminate good practice in this area. if we fail to treat refugees as human beings, sooner or later, our own human dignity is at stake.
mr. president, this whole discussion about the future of bosnia and herzegovina is very strange. we hear here that the dayton accords are broken, that they paralyze the country, and so on. all of that is true, but let me remind you how we got into this situation. first we force bosnia to accept the dayton agreement, and then we complain that bosnia is not working because of that agreement. well, that's great. the problem is not that bosnians do not want to implement the reforms necessary for joining the eu. the problem is that there is a strong resistance in bosnia from nationalists who do not want to see the country in the european community, and we, the european union, have not done enough to help bosnia to deal with these nationalists. reforms are, of course, necessary. these funds that the commission has approved are very important funds, but if the european union does not help to implement these reforms, we will soon be complaining again that the reforms have not been implemented and that bosnia's european future is even further away than it is today.
the rapporteur. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to begin by thanking all the shadow rapporteurs who have been very helpful in the preparation of this report. it has been several years since parliament presented its last arms export report, and my ambition is now to resume the annual review. not least because we live in a completely new security policy environment with major changes in the world around us. we have both wars and conflicts in our neighborhood and related armed violence at home. in this context, parliament has an increasingly important role to play in its review of the compliance of member states with the common framework for arms exports. the report is therefore based on three pillars: the changed security situation, the application of the common regulatory framework and openness and transparency. the other day, less than a month after the terrible attacks in paris, amnesty international released a report, this report. the report details how daesh has been getting its weapons. the weapons involved were exported to iraq during the war against iran and later to the iraqi army, and then became too easy a prey for daesh when they captured large parts of all the weapons of the iraqi defence. the report mentions a number of european arms exports from, for example, germany, belgium, the czech republic and croatia. my home country sweden is not mentioned, but given that swedish war material has also been exported to iraq, both via licensed products and indirectly via the united states, i consider it highly likely that they have appeared or may appear in daesh's ruthless war against all dissenters. the question we must ask ourselves is why daesh has so many european weapons and whether the member states have not lived up to the common position? we are now in a situation where our own weapons, once legally sold, are completely out of our control, and we do not know who has them, what they are to be used for, or whether they risk being turned against us. for many years during peace, virtually all arms-exporting countries sold military equipment to the dictators of the middle east and north africa without any major concern about the lack of respect for human rights or the situation in the country as such. the dictators were seen as stable, and they became an important market for european war materials. the area is now on fire, with many individual member states holding long contracts and promises of successive deliveries for many years to come. we can therefore conclude that the risk assessments have not worked, nor the end-user controls, but also that the member states have stretched the regulatory framework to the limit in order to sell. today raif badawi has been awarded the sakharov prize, and in this house we have heard about the terrible punishments inflicted on badawi and many other saudis for the mere expression of their opinions, but despite the total lack of respect for human rights, saudi arabia is still the country that gets to buy the most european war material, although it is clearly not only using it for its own defence but also now in a war of aggression in neighbouring yemen, where civilians are suffering badly. our role as members of parliament is to check that the member states are complying with the common position. a weapon is not just any item. weapons that were exported 10, 20, 30 years ago are as dangerous today as they were when they were sold. our role is also to suggest improvements. today we propose, among other things, a stricter risk assessment focusing on the situation in the recipient country, better follow-up with end-user inspections and, not least, greater transparency and democratic scrutiny of arms exports.
the rapporteur. president. - the debate is closed. yes, it is obvious that we have different views on this issue, and that was also the case when we started work on the report, but i felt that together with the shadow rapporteurs we were able to find a common line, and there was also very strong support in the committee when we voted on the report. since then, something has obviously happened, and what i find most surprising in this debate is that some of those who have been up and speaking on behalf of their parties have not really spoken about what we are really talking about. we are talking about the control of legal arms exports, the control that member states actually exercise over companies located in their countries. we are not talking about illegal trade, we are talking about legal trade, and that is where parliament has a role to play, because it is here that we can go in and check and make sure that the weapons that we ourselves are selling, so to speak, do not fall into the wrong hands for various reasons. the illegal arms trade, we deal with it with a completely different set of rules. this is not the regulatory framework we can use for that. one of the speakers was clearly not talking at all about the control of arms exports of legal weapons but was talking about the new commission proposal which deals with a completely different issue, that of hunting weapons, shooting sports, etc. so that we are talking about two completely different things. i hope, however, that when we vote tomorrow we will be able to get broad enough support for the report in this house and that we can move on so that we will also have a new annual report next year, so that this will be the annual review that parliament is making of the legal sale of arms, and for which we have common rules.
writer. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. yes, the developments in malaysia in terms of the restrictions on freedom of expression and other fundamental freedoms, they are alarming. the government is doing its best to silence critics and the political opposition, and this is why parliament must today deplore the human rights situation being put at risk, and the legislation allowing for the targeting of representatives of civil society, academics, the media and the opposition. we therefore call on the government in the resolution to immediately release the political prisoners and ordinary people who are merely exercising their freedom of expression but who in no way pose a danger or risk to the security of the country, and also to immediately stop the increasing persecution and violence directed against lgbtq people in the country. it is important that we adopt the urgent resolution on the political situation in malaysia today. we must show the regime that freedom of speech is a fundamental human right that cannot be restricted anywhere. this is the eu's most important mission to protect fundamental rights both at home in europe and in the rest of the world.

‒ i have cast my vote on the report on the annual report. the report highlights good things, including surrogacy, the rights of women and lgbtq people, and the importance of binding human rights clauses in trade policy. the report, however, places far too much emphasis on a common foreign and security policy and supports the khartoum process. the report also stresses the importance of the eu's external borders and calls for the burden of refugees to be lifted. at a time when the eu does not respect the right to asylum, when the eu is deporting thousands of people to life-threatening situations, when the eu is protecting borders instead of people, i would need to see stronger language in support of human rights and an emphasis on solidarity rather than borders to support the report.
in writing. an important report on the eu's role in influencing human rights issues. i voted against the general condemnation of surrogacy and i voted for the recognition of western sahara as a sovereign state and i voted for the whole report.
- i've put my voice down. the report is characterised by a critical tone towards the arms exports of the eu countries, which contribute to the ongoing conflicts and wars in our world. but i think that the report would be even more critical and would make even clearer the importance of the eu not only in words but also in concrete actions in favour of peace and détente. some of the points in the report stress the need for a common foreign, security and defence policy on the part of the eu. i am opposed to this, because it is part of the construction of a european federal superpower. i defend my own country, and other countries, the right to freedom of association and an independent foreign policy for peace, détente and disarmament.
balanced and good report on eu arms exports. however, i voted against a new eu arms export control authority and made it clear that the new terrorism laws introduced against illegal weapons should not regulate legal weapons used by hunters and sport shooters.
in writing. ‒ the sweden democrats support the european code of conduct on arms exports and recognise the special responsibility of arms exporting states (the 8 eu criteria for arms export). sweden as a country has also signed the wassenaar arrangement on export of dual-use goods and technologies (based on consensus). we also have a rigorous national export control system. according to article 346 in teu, we also maintain the right to protect the national military industry. the sd opinion on further strengthening of the eu control mechanisms on member states’ arms- and dual-use goods export is that this control is mainly up to the common position, for member states to implement nationally.
in writing. ‒ the sweden democrats support the european code of conduct on arms exports and recognise the special responsibility of arms exporting states (the 8 eu criteria for arms export). sweden as a country has also signed the wassenaar arrangement on export of dual-use goods and technologies (based on consensus). we also have a rigorous national export control system. according to article 346 in teu, we also maintain the right to protect the national military industry. the sd opinion on further strengthening of the eu control mechanisms on member states’ arms- and dual-use goods export is that this control is mainly up to the common position, for member states to implement nationally.
